_History of Life and Death._ [Page number:] 91

# Major Observations.

It is difficult to find a certain rule for the lon-
gevity and brevity of life in animals,
due to the neglect of observations,
and the complexity of causes:
We will note a few things.
<margin>1</margin> More long-lived species are found among
birds, than
among <term>quadrupeds</term> <note>four-footed animals</note>; (such as
the Eagle, Vulture, Kite,
Pelican, Raven, Crow,
Swan, Goose, Stork,
Crane, Ibis, and Parrot).

<summary>Bacon notes the difficulty of establishing a universal rule for animal longevity due to a lack of scientific data, but observes that birds generally live longer than four-footed mammals.</summary>

<keywords>longevity, animal life spans, birds, quadrupeds, Francis Bacon, natural history, ornithology</keywords>

[...parrots], wood-pigeons, etc.) although
they reach full size within a year,
and are of smaller mass. Cer-

harshness of the sky; and since they

(as has been stated elsewhere)
is a mixture of being carried <note>original: *gestatione*; here referring to the way a bird is supported by the air, similar to being carried in a carriage or boat.</note>

[does growth occur] in the womb <note>original: *vtero*. The text breaks here; it likely begins a comparison between the development of birds in eggs versus the development of mammals in a womb.</note>

**History of Life and Death.** [Page number:] **93**

...of the mothers, the beginnings of birds <note>Bacon refers here to the earliest stages of embryonic development</note> suffer from compression or a scarcity of nourishment; this is because eggs are laid in succession. However, we believe the primary reason [for their longevity] is that birds are formed more from the substance of the mother than the father; consequently, they acquire a spirit <note>original: "spiritum"; in Bacon's theory, 'spirit' was a subtle, material substance that governed life and decay</note> that is less sharp and fiery. <note>A 'less fiery' spirit was thought to consume the body's natural moisture more slowly, thus prolonging life.</note>

<margin>2</margin>
It might be proposed that animals created more from the substance of the mother than from the father tend to be longer-lived, just as has been said of birds.

[Signature mark:] **G 2** [Catchword:] **Also-** <note>original catchword: "Eti-" for "Etiam"</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that birds live longer because they are formed primarily from maternal substance, resulting in a 'cooler' vital spirit that preserves the body.</summary>

The principal seat of the spirits is undoubtedly in the head; and although this is commonly referred only to the **animal spirits** <term>animal spirits: In early modern physiology, these were believed to be subtle, gas-like substances that moved through the nerves to control sensation and movement; the term comes from 'anima', meaning soul or life-force</term>, nevertheless, that same rule applies to all spirits. ->[Header] History of Life and Death. Page 99<-

...so that either a greater abundance of them <note>the vital spirits</note>, or a greater burning and sharpness, greatly shortens life. Therefore, we believe a major cause of **Longevity** in **Birds** is that, in proportion to the size of their bodies, they have such small heads; indeed, we believe that even **Men** who have very large skulls live for a shorter duration.

We judge that **Gestation** <note>original: "Gestationem"; the practice of being carried in a litter, carriage, or boat</note> (as we have previously <margin>8</margin> noted) surpasses every other kind of movement in promoting a long life;

<summary>Bacon posits that smaller head size contributes to a longer life by reducing the consumption of vital spirits and argues that passive transport is the most healthful form of motion.</summary>

<meta>This page is part of Francis Bacon's "History of Life and Death," specifically focusing on the physical factors that contribute to longevity.</meta>

102 	 *History of Life and Death.*

The quantity itself contributes greatly to the preservation of the whole <note>The author argues that larger objects resist decay or change better than smaller ones simply due to their volume.</note>: for indeed, a large fire takes a longer time to be extinguished; a small portion of water evaporates more quickly; a tree trunk does not dry out as fast as a twig; and so, generally (I speak of species, not of individuals), animals that are larger in mass <term>mass (original Latin: *mole*), referring to physical bulk or size</term> are longer-lived than tiny ones, unless some other powerful cause prevents it.

->NOURISH-<- <note>original: "ALIMEN-", a catchword for the following page, likely "Alimentum" (Nourishment).</note>

<summary>This page discusses the principle that larger physical mass generally leads to greater longevity in animals and objects, as larger volumes resist environmental changes like drying or extinguishing more effectively than smaller ones.</summary>
<keywords>longevity, physical mass, preservation, Francis Bacon, animal species, decay</keywords>

->**The Way of Nourishing.**<-

->**History** <note>In Francis Bacon’s scientific method, a "History" is a structured collection of observations and facts used to build a larger scientific theory.</note><-

<margin>Regarding Article 4, 1</margin>
**Nourishment** <term>Alimentum: any substance that provides the material for growth or repair in a living body</term>, in relation to that which is nourished, must be of an inferior nature and of a simpler substance. Plants are nourished by Earth and Water; Animals by Plants; Humans by Animals. There are also carnivorous Animals, and Man himself consumes Plants as part of his **Nourishment**; but Man, and carnivorous Animals, are nourished with difficulty by Plants alone; they can perhaps be sustained by Fruits and Seeds that have been cooked by Fire, <note>Bacon suggests that cooking serves as a form of "pre-digestion," using external heat to break down complex plant matter so it can be more easily assimilated by the human body.</note>

<summary>Bacon outlines a biological hierarchy of diet, arguing that organisms must consume "simpler" substances and noting that humans and carnivores require animal protein or fire-cooked plants for proper health.</summary>
<keywords>nourishment, diet, Francis Bacon, natural history, digestion, biology, cooking, hierarchy of nature</keywords>

*History of Life and Death.* <margin>107</margin>
__________________________________________________________________
[...nourishment], whether from the inside out; nevertheless, it should be noted that trees and plants are nourished more through their bark and outer layers than through their pith and <margin>10</margin> innermost parts. For if they are stripped of their bark all around, even for a short <margin>8</margin> distance, they no longer live. Similarly, the blood in the veins of animals nourishes the flesh located beneath them no less than the flesh above them.

In every act of nourishment <margin>6</margin> there is a double action: extrusion and attraction. The first of these proceeds from an internal <margin>e</margin> function, while the second proceeds from an external one.
<note>Extruſio (extrusion) refers to the body pushing nutrients toward the tissues, while Attractio (attraction) refers to the tissues pulling those nutrients in.</note>

Vegetables <note>In this period, "vegetables" referred to all plant life, including trees.</note> assimilate <margin>7</margin> their food simply, without excretion. Indeed, gums and "tears" <note>original: lachrymæ; the resin or sap that beads on the surface of a plant</note> are results of an overabundance rather than waste or excrement.
            H               Excre-

-># History of Life and Death. 109<-
***

...grow more vigorously, unless they are covered with earth.

In animals, there are stages of nourishment according to age. For the fetus in the womb, the "maternal juice" <note>the fluids providing nutrients to the embryo</note> is sufficient; from birth, milk; afterward, solid foods and drinks. In old age, coarser and more flavorful foods are generally preferred.

<margin>Instruction</margin> Above all else, it is vital for the present investigation to diligently and carefully inquire whether nourishment might be achieved from the outside—or at least not through the mouth. Certainly, milk baths are prescribed for those suffering from wasting diseases <note>Latin original: *marasmus*, a condition of severe malnutrition and weight loss</note> and emaciation. Nor is there a shortage of physicians who believe that some nourishment can be provided through enemas <note>Latin: *clysteria*</note>. This matter must be thoroughly explored; for if nourishment can be achieved either from

H 2                     Outside,

->**LONGEVITY**
and Brevity of life in
Man.<-

### History.

<margin>Regarding Articles 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, and 11. 1</margin>
Before the Flood, Holy Scripture relates that men lived for several hundreds of years; yet none of the Patriarchs <note>original: "Patrum"; referring to the biblical figures mentioned in the book of Genesis</note> completed his thousandth year. Nor was this length of life due to Gra- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "Gratia" (Grace) or "Gradu" (degree)</note>

<summary>This page discusses compensating for the weakened digestion of the elderly through external medical aids and begins a historical survey of human longevity, starting with the centuries-long lifespans of the biblical patriarchs before the Great Flood.</summary>
<keywords>Longevity, digestion, concoction, aging, biblical patriarchs, antediluvian, Francis Bacon</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on whether the extreme longevity of the biblical patriarchs was a result of divine favor ("Holy Lineage") or natural factors, by comparing the descendants of Seth and Cain.</meta>

[Header]
History of Life and Death. [Page 111]

[Main Text]
...can be attributed to Grace, or to the Holy Lineage <note>original: "Lineæ Sanctæ"; referring to the descendants of Seth, who were considered the spiritually favored line compared to the descendants of Cain.</note>; since eleven generations of the Patriarchs are recounted before the Great Flood, whereas there are only eight generations of the sons of Adam through Cain; so that the offspring of Cain might even seem to be the longer-lived. 

Indeed, this longevity collapsed by half immediately after the Flood, but only in those born afterward; for Noah, who had been born before the Flood, matched the age of his ancestors; and Shem reached his six-hundredth year. Then, after three generations from the Flood, the life of men was reduced to nearly a fourth part of the primitive age <note>The "primitive age" refers to the era of the world before the Flood.</note>; namely, to about two hundred years.

<margin>2</margin>

--- Page 22 ---
The standard size for a dining room table that seats 8 people depends primarily on the **shape** of the table. To ensure everyone has enough elbow room (about 24–30 inches per person), here are the standard dimensions:

### 1. Rectangular Tables (Most Common)
A rectangular table is the most popular choice for seating eight.
*   **Standard Length:** 72 to 96 inches (approx. 183 to 244 cm).
*   **Standard Width:** 36 to 42 inches (approx. 91 to 107 cm).
*   **Seating Arrangement:** Usually 3 people on each long side and 1 person at each head of the table. 
*   **Note:** A **72-inch** table is a "tight" 8, while a **96-inch** table allows for generous spacing and larger chairs.

### 2. Round Tables
Round tables are great for conversation but require a large room because they take up significant floor space.
*   **Standard Diameter:** 60 to 72 inches (approx. 152 to 183 cm).
*   **Seating Arrangement:** 8 people around the circumference.
*   **Note:** A **60-inch** table fits 8 people closely; a **72-inch** table fits 8 very comfortably (and can often fit up to 10).

### 3. Square Tables
Square tables offer an intimate dining experience where everyone can see each other, but they require a very large square dining area.
*   **Standard Size:** 60 to 66 inches (approx. 152 to 168 cm) on all sides.
*   **Seating Arrangement:** 2 people per side.

### 4. Oval Tables
Oval tables provide a similar surface area to rectangular tables but take up slightly less visual space due to the rounded corners.
*   **Standard Length:** 72 to 96 inches (approx. 183 to 244 cm).
*   **Standard Width:** 36 to 42 inches (approx. 91 to 107 cm).

---

### Important Considerations for Fit

*   **Elbow Room:** For a "comfortable" feel, aim for **30 inches of width per person**. For a "standard" feel, **24 inches** is the minimum.
*   **Table Depth:** Ensure the table is at least **36 inches wide**. Anything narrower makes it difficult to place serving platters or centerpieces in the middle once place settings are laid out.
*   **The "Rule of 3 Feet":** To allow people to pull out their chairs and walk behind seated guests, you should have at least **36 inches (3 feet) of clearance** between the edge of the table and the wall or any other furniture.
*   **Leg Placement:** If the table has four legs at the corners, ensure the distance between the legs is wide enough to tuck in the chairs. Pedestal tables are often better for maximizing legroom and squeezing in extra guests.

**Summary Recommendation:**
If you want a comfortable fit for 8 adults with room for food in the middle, go with a **96" x 40" rectangular table** or a **66" round table**.

# History of Life and Death | 129

Augusta <note>Livia Drusilla, the first Roman Empress</note>, the wife of Caesar Augustus and mother of Tiberius. Indeed, if the life of Augustus was a play (which he himself suggested when, lying on his deathbed, he instructed his friends to grant him a *Plaudite* <note>original: "Plaudite"; the traditional request for applause at the end of a Roman theatrical performance</note> after he had breathed his last), then certainly Livia was the greatest actress. She balanced her life so well, showing obedience toward her husband and a certain power and dominance toward her son. She was a woman of graciousness, yet of a matronly bearing; she was industrious and tenacious in her hold on power. 

But Junia, the wife of Gaius Cassius and sister of Marcus Brutus <note>Cassius and Brutus were the leaders of the plot to assassinate Julius Caesar</note>, also lived to be ninety years old; she survived for sixty-four years after the Battle of Philippi. She was a woman of great spirit and blessed with wealth, though saddened by the catastrophe that befell her husband and close relatives, and by her long widowhood; nevertheless, she remained highly respected.

15 . | The seventy-sixth year of our Lord is memorable, occurring in the time of the Emperor Vespasian <note>Roman Emperor from 69–79 AD</note>, in which records of Longevity are found, as if in the Public Annals <term>Fasti: official Roman chronological records or calendars</term>; for in that year a Census was conducted (and a census provides the most reliable authority and information regarding the ages of the populace). In that region of Italy which lies between the Apennines and the Po River <note>original: "Padum"</note>, there were found one hundred and twenty-four men who had reached or surpassed their hundredth year; namely: fifty-four men of one hundred years; fifty-seven men of one hundred and ten years; two men of one hundred and twenty-five years; [and] of one hundred and thirty years, men... <note>The text breaks off here at the beginning of the word "Homines" (men/people). The total of 124 suggests more entries followed on the next page.</note>

134	*History of Life and Death.*
________________________________________
In his thirtieth year, he <note>Referring to Augustus Caesar, the subject of the preceding text.</note> suffered a grave and dangerous illness, so much so that his recovery was despaired of. This illness was cured by *Antonius Musa the Physician*; for while other doctors had applied "hot" remedies—believing them appropriate for the disease—Musa treated it by the opposite method using "cold" remedies <note>In ancient medicine, illnesses and treatments were categorized as "hot," "cold," "moist," or "dry." Musa famously cured Augustus with cold baths and cold drinks.</note>. This perhaps contributed to his long life. *Tiberius* <note>Augustus’s successor as Emperor.</note> lived two years longer than Augustus; he was a *man of slow jaws* <note>original: "Vir lentis maxillis" — a phrase Augustus used to describe Tiberius’s slow, deliberate, and perhaps hesitant way of speaking.</note> (as Augustus used to say), slow indeed in speech, but physically robust. He was bloodthirsty and fond of drink, and someone who even turned his lusts into a daily regimen; yet he was a careful guardian of his own health, being accustomed to say that it was a foolish man who, after thirty years of life, would still need to consult a physician <note>Tiberius famously believed that by the age of thirty, a man should understand his own constitution well enough to know what helps or harms him without professional advice.</note>.

<summary>This page details how the physician Antonius Musa used "cold" treatments to save the life of Augustus Caesar and describes the robust but debauched character of Emperor Tiberius, who believed adults should be their own doctors.</summary>
<keywords>Augustus Caesar, Antonius Musa, Tiberius, ancient medicine, longevity, Roman Emperors, health habits</keywords>

He was certainly extraordinary; held as **divine** among the Pagans, and as a **Magician** among **Christians**. He was a Pythagorean in his way of life <note>Referring to Apollonius of Tyana, a famous neo-Pythagorean philosopher and teacher</note>, a great traveler, flourishing also in great glory, and worshipped as if he were a deity. However, toward the end of his life, he suffered accusations and insults, from which he nevertheless escaped unharmed in one way or another. Yet, lest his **longevity** be attributed solely to the **Pythagorean diet**, but rather seem to have drawn something from his lineage, it is noted that **his grandfather also** lived one hundred and thirty years. It is a certain fact that **Quintus Metellus** lived beyond a hundred years; and after successfully administering his **consular commands**, he was appointed **High Priest** <term>Pontifex Maximus | the chief high priest of the College of Pontiffs in ancient Rome</term> when already an old man, and oversaw the sacred rites for twenty-two [years]...

<summary>This page discusses the longevity of Apollonius of Tyana, attributing it to both his Pythagorean lifestyle and his genetics, and begins the account of the long-lived Roman statesman Quintus Metellus.</summary>

...is that concerning *Cornaro of Venice* <note>Alvise (Luigi) Cornaro (c. 1484–1566), a Venetian nobleman who wrote "The Art of Living Long."</note>, who, having a sickly body in his youth, first began to measure his food and drink to a specific weight for the sake of his health. *Guillaume Postel* <note>A famous French polymath, linguist, and mystic (1510–1581).</note>, a *Frenchman* of our own age, lived to nearly his hundred and twentieth year; even the tips of the beard on his upper lip remained somewhat black, and were not entirely gray. 22 | The lifespans about which people tell fables—those of **Nymphs** and **Air-Demons**, who were said to be mortal in body but exceptionally long-lived (a notion accepted by both ancient superstition and even some recent credulity)—we regard as nothing more than **fables** and **dreams**. This is especially true since such things agree neither with philosophy <note>In Bacon's time, "philosophy" usually referred to "natural philosophy," what we now call science.</note> nor with religion. And so, regarding the history of longevity in humans, examined through individuals or specific examples, let these findings suffice. Now we shall move on to observations arranged by chapters.

Although there were formerly men of *Gigantic Stature* <note>During the Renaissance and early modern period, large fossilized bones (often from prehistoric megafauna) were frequently misinterpreted as the remains of "giant" humans from antiquity.</note> (such as have been found for certain in *Sicily* and elsewhere, in ancient Sepulchers and Caverns), nevertheless, for nearly three millennia now—to which period fairly certain memory extends—nothing of the kind persists in those same places; although even this matter undergoes certain changes through civil morals and customs, just as that other matter does. | 24 | In colder and *Northern* regions, people generally live longer than in *Warmer* ones. This must be the case because the skin is more tightly bound; the juices of the body are less likely to evaporate; and the <term>spirits<note>In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" were considered thin, invisible fluids that acted as the bridge between the soul and the physical body, responsible for animation and decay.</note></term> themselves are less sharp in consuming the body's resources and more efficient in repairing them. Furthermore, the air (being only moderately heated by the sun’s rays) is less "predatory" or draining to the body's moisture. However, directly under the *Equator*, where the sun passes overhead and there is a double winter and summer, and where there is a greater equality between the length of days and nights, people also live quite a long time (provided other factors do not interfere); such as in *Peru* and *Taprobana* <note>The ancient name for the island of Sri Lanka.</note>. |
| 25 | *Islanders* generally live longer than those living in the *interior* of the mainland... |

<summary>Bacon argues that cold northern climates and specific equatorial regions favor longevity by preserving the body’s internal "spirits" and moisture from being drained by the sun.</summary>

*Marshes and their expanses,* 27
especially those spread out over flat land, are favorable to natives but harmful to newcomers in terms of the prolongation or shortening of life. And what might seem surprising: *marshes* flooded periodically with salt water are less healthy than those with fresh water.

An experiment might be conducted using a **Fleece of Wool**, by exposing it to the open air for a period of several days to see if its weight increases less than usual <note>Bacon suggests that wool's tendency to absorb moisture from the air can be used to measure the "dryness" or "predatory" nature of the atmosphere.</note>. Another could involve a **Piece of Meat** remaining unrotted for a longer duration. Another might use a **Weather Glass** <note>original: "Vitro Calendari"; an early scientific instrument, similar to a thermometer, used to observe changes in air temperature or pressure</note> showing less fluctuation in a given time. <margin>8 1/2</margin> Let there be further inquiry into these and similar matters.

<margin>30</margin>
Regarding **Longevity**, we must consider not only the **Goodness** or **Purity** of the air, but also its **Consistency**. A landscape with a variety of **Hills and Valleys**, though pleasing to the sight and the senses, is suspect for longevity; however, a **Plain** that is moderately dry—yet not overly sterile or sandy, nor entirely without trees and shade—contributes to a long <margin>e 5</margin> life... <note>The text ends mid-sentence with "Diutur-", the beginning of the word for "duration" or "long-lastingness."</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon outlines empirical methods for testing air quality and argues that consistent, moderate climates in flat plains are better for long life than varied, mountainous terrain.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, air quality, experimental method, weather glass, climate and health, geography</keywords>

...more advantageous to the longevity of Life.
   <margin>31</margin> *Inequality of the Air* (as has already been stated) in one’s *Place of Residence* is bad; but a *Change of Air* during *Travel*, once a person has become accustomed to it, is good. For this reason, even great *Travelers* have often been *Long-lived*; and similarly, those *Long-lived* individuals who have spent their lives perpetually in the same place within their *Small Cottages* <note>The Latin "tuguriolis" implies humble, small dwellings or huts.</note> have also flourished. For *Accustomed Air* consumes <note>Bacon believed that familiar environments caused the "vital spirits" to remain calm, thus "consuming" the body's moisture and energy more slowly.</note> the body less, while *Changed Air* nourishes and repairs it more.

   <margin>32</margin> Just as the *Sequence and Number of Generations* <note>original: "Successionum"</note> contributes nothing to the Length or Brevity of life (as we have already said), so the immediate *Condition of the Parents*, as much on the *Father’s* side as the *Mother’s*, undoubtedly carries great weight. For some are generated...

...longevity <note>original: "tatem"; completing "longævitatem" from the previous page</note> is difficult; and all the more difficult because perhaps those things which one might think best turn out to the contrary. For that very vigor in the act of reproduction, which produces children with robust and agile bodies, will be less useful for longevity, because of the sharpness and inflammation of the <term>spirits</term> <note>In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" (spiritus) were invisible, vapor-like substances that moved through the nerves and blood to govern life and thought. Many balding men <note>The Latin *caluastri* refers specifically to those who are beginning to go bald or are somewhat bald.</note> have been long-lived; also, to **go gray** quickly (however much **gray hair** <note>original: *Canicies*</note> may seem a precursor of oncoming Old Age) is a deceptive thing; since not a few who turned **gray** prematurely lived for a long time afterward. <margin>36</margin> **Hairiness of the Upper Parts** is a sign of a shorter life; and those with hairy chests, who are almost maned, are less long-lived: but **hairiness** of the lower parts, such as the thighs and shins, is a sign of long life.

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's discussion on the effects of different dietary regimens on the body's "spirits" and longevity, transitioning from the specific qualities of food to the lifestyle habits that must accompany them.</meta>

*History of Life and Death.* [page] 177
***
A *fuller* diet <note>original: *Plenior*</note> provides abundant nourishment, from which it repairs the body more; a *middle* diet provides neither. For where extremes are harmful, the middle path is best; but where extremes are beneficial, the middle is worth almost nothing. Now, for that *stricter* *diet*, *wakefulness* <note>remaining awake or alert</note> is also appropriate, lest the few spirits <term>vital spirits: in early modern science, these were subtle, air-like fluids believed to circulate through the body to sustain life and movement</term> be overwhelmed by too much sleep; likewise moderate *exercise*, lest they be dissipated; and *abstinence from Venus* <note>original: *Veneris Abstinentia*; a common euphemism for refraining from sexual activity</note>, lest they be exhausted. But for a *more plentiful diet*, on the contrary, more generous *sleep* is suitable, more frequent *exercise*, and the timely *use of Venus*. *Baths* and *ointments* (as they were commonly used) were adapted more for luxury <note>original: *Delicijs*</note> than for the prolonging of life. Truly, concerning all these things, when [we come] to the *Inquiry*
[footer] M 4 [space] second-

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that while a moderate diet is often praised, extreme diets (either very strict or very full) are more effective for bodily restoration when paired with specific habits regarding sleep, exercise, and sexual activity. He dismisses contemporary use of baths and ointments as mere luxuries rather than medicinal tools for longevity.</summary>

<keywords>Francis Bacon, diet, vital spirits, longevity, exercise, sleep, sexual health, history of medicine, 17th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's discourse on the "History of Life and Death," specifically discussing the "Intentions" or systematic methods for prolonging human life through lifestyle and diet.</meta>

->178 | History of Life and Death.<-
***

...when we have come to the specific *Intentions* <note>original: "Intentiones"; Bacon uses this term to describe his systematic goals or scientific categories for preserving health</note>, we shall speak more accurately. In the meantime, that advice of the physician Celsus <note>Aulus Cornelius Celsus, a Roman medical writer of the 1st century AD</note>—who was not only learned but also wise—is not to be despised. He commands variety and alternation in one’s diet, but always with an inclination toward the more indulgent side: namely, that one should sometimes accustom oneself to staying awake, and at other times indulge in sleep, but more often in sleep; likewise, one should sometimes fast, and sometimes feast, but feast more often; sometimes one should strenuously apply oneself to mental exertions, and at other times make use of relaxation, but more often relaxation. It is certainly beyond doubt that a well-ordered <term>Diet</term> <note>original: "Diæta"; in the 17th century, this referred to a person’s entire daily regimen, including food, exercise, and sleep</note> plays a primary role in prolonging life; nor is it fit—

<summary>Bacon cites the Roman physician Celsus to argue that a healthy lifestyle for longevity should involve a balance of activities, generally leaning toward rest and nourishment rather than over-exertion.</summary>

<keywords>Francis Bacon, Celsus, longevity, diet, lifestyle, sleep, fasting, mental exertion, moderation</keywords>

*History of Life and Death.* [Page number] 179

...nor have I ever seen anyone very long-lived who, when questioned about their *diet*, had not observed some peculiar habit; some one thing, others another. Indeed, I remember a certain old man over a hundred years of age, who was produced as a witness concerning a certain ancient <term>prescription<note>In a legal context, a 'prescription' refers to a right or claim established by long-standing custom or continued use.</note></term>. When his testimony was finished, he was asked familiarly by the judge what he had done to live so long; he replied (unexpectedly, and to the laughter of those listening), "*By eating before I was hungry, and by drinking before I was thirsty.*" <note>original Latin: "Edendo, antequàm esurirem, & Potando, antequàm sitirem."</note> But concerning these matters (as has been said), we shall speak later.

*The religious life*, and life in sacred service, seems to contribute to longevity; in this kind of life, there is leisure;

**Warning.** | Since we are now discussing things that can be incorporated into a person's diet, those **"Burning Waters"** <note>original: "Aquæ Ardentiores"; these are distilled spirits or strong alcohols</note> and **Chemical Oils**—which, as one of those triflers <note>original: "Nugatoribus"; Bacon likely refers to the followers of Paracelsus or mystical alchemists whose theories he often found unscientific</note> says, are under the influence of the **Planet Mars** and possess a furious and destructive power—must be rejected. Furthermore, those spices which are sharp and biting to the tongue should also be avoided. Instead, we must determine how waters and liquors can be composed from the ingredients mentioned previously. These should not be those watery, <term>phlegmatic</term> <note>In the medical theory of the time, "phlegmatic" referred to substances that were cold, moist, and lacked "spirit" or vital energy</note> distilled liquids, nor again those "burning" spirits made from wine <note>brandy or ethanol</note>, but rather liquids that are more tempered, yet nonetheless lively and breathing out a gentle, healing vapor.
6 | We hesitate regarding the practice of **frequent bloodletting**, and whether it contributes to a long life. We are currently of the opinion that it does indeed do so... <note>Bacon's theory was that removing old blood encouraged the body to produce "fresh" and more vigorous blood, though he remained cautious about its overall impact on vitality.</note>

As for **Exercise**; an **idle life** clearly renders the **12**
flesh **soft and easily dissipated**: but **robust**
**exercise** (provided there is no excessive sweating or 
exhaustion) renders it hard and compact. 
Also, exercise in **cold waters**, such as **swimming**, is
very good; and generally **exercise in the open air** <note>original: "sub Dio", literally "under the sky"</note> is
better than **under a roof**. 
Regarding **frictions** <note>original: "Fricationibus", referring to therapeutic rubbing or massage intended to stimulate the skin</note> (which is a **type of exercise**), **13**
however, because they call forth nourishment more 
than they harden the flesh, we shall inquire into 
them later in their proper place.

Now indeed, since the **hardness of the juices** <note>original: "Duritie Succorum"; the "juices" or humors were believed to be the fundamental fluids of the body</note> has **14**
been discussed, we must come to their **oiliness**
or **dewiness** <note>original: "Roscidationem". Bacon uses this term to describe a state where the body's fluids are rich and sustaining like dew, rather than thin or watery</note> of the—

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's physiological inquiry into the preservation of the body's "juices" (vital fluids) to extend life. He is comparing the method of "hardening" tissues versus "moistening" them.</meta>

294 | History of *Life & Death.*

of them; which is a more perfect and more powerful *Intention* <note>Bacon uses 'Intention' to mean a specific medical strategy or therapeutic goal</note>, than *Hardening*; because it has no *Inconvenience*, nor any complicated *Evil* <note>In this context, 'evil' refers to a physiological disadvantage or harmful side effect</note> attached to it: For all things which pertain to the *Hardness of the Juices* <note>The 'succi' or internal fluids that Bacon believed nourished the body's organs</note> are of such a kind that, while they prevent the *Consumption of Nourishment*, they also hinder its *Restoration*; from which it happens that for the *Length of life*, these same things both help and hinder; But those things which pertain to the *Dew-like Moistening of the Juices* <note>original: 'Roscidationem Succorum'; a process of making the body's fluids thick and moist like dew to prevent them from drying out</note>, assist in both ways; since they render the *Nourishment* both less *Prone to Dissipation* <note>Less likely to be evaporated or wasted by the body's internal heat</note>, and more *Capable of Restoration.*

15 When, however, we say that the *Juice of the Body* ought to become *Dew-like*, and *Rich*; it must be noted that we do not understand this to mean *Fat-
ness,* <note>Bacon distinguishes between 'rich' juices that sustain the organs and the mere accumulation of 'fat' or adipose tissue</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that keeping the body's vital juices "dew-like" and "rich" is a superior strategy for longevity than "hardening" the tissues, as hardening prevents the body from repairing itself.</summary>

Nor again should anyone suppose that the oils or fats of foods, or marrow, generate substances similar to themselves to satisfy our goal; for things that have once been <term>perfected</term> <note>original: *perfecta*; referring to substances that have already reached their final state of "concoction" or digestion, meaning they cannot be easily re-processed by the body into a new form.</note> do not move backward; rather, foods should be of such a kind that, after digestion and maturation, only then do they generate an oiliness within the bodily juices.

**18** | For the <term>moistening of the body<note>original: "irrorationem corporis"; a concept in Bacon's physiology referring to the internal "sprinkling" or "bedewing" of the organs and tissues with nourishing fluids to keep them supple.</note></term>, *roasted meats* or those *baked in an oven* are better than *boiled* ones. Indeed, any *preparation of foods* using *water* is unsuitable; furthermore, we see that *oil* is extracted more abundantly from *dry bodies* than from *moist* ones. <note>Bacon argues that water-based cooking leaches out the essential, life-preserving oils from food, whereas dry heat preserves them.</note>

**19** | Generally, for the *moistening of the body*, it is beneficial to make frequent use of *sweet things*, such as *sugar, honey, sweet almonds, pine nuts, pistachios, dates, raisins, currants* <note>original: "vuarum corinthi"; literally "grapes of Corinth," from which the word "currant" is derived.</note>, *figs*, and the like. On the contrary, all *acidic* things...

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's dietary prescriptions for longevity, specifically focusing on substances that promote the body's internal moisture, following his discussion on the qualities of various foods.</meta>

[Page number] 297

[Header] *History of Life and Death.*

[Main text]
Sour things, and those that are excessively salty or excessively pungent, are opposed to the generation of <term>dewy juice</term> <note>original: "Succi Roscidi"; Bacon's term for the vital, restorative moisture that keeps tissues youthful and prevents them from drying out.</note>.

<margin>20</margin> Nor should we be thought to favor the *Manichaeans* <note>An ancient religious group known for their strict vegetarianism and avoidance of wine.</note> and their diet if we say that various *seeds*, *nuts*, and *roots* ought to be frequent in foods or their seasonings; since all *bread* (and *bread* is the foundation of food) is made either from *seeds* or from *roots*.

<margin>21</margin> But above all, the *nature of the drink*, which is the vehicle for food, contributes most to the *moistening of the body*: Therefore, those *drinks* should be in use which, without any *sharpness* or *sourness*, are nevertheless subtle, such as *wines* that (as the *old woman* in *Plautus* <note>A reference to the Roman playwright Plautus; the "old woman" character often praises wine that has lost its harsh "bite" through age.</note> says) are "toothless" with *age*—

[Catchword] toothless—

<summary>Bacon argues that avoiding harsh, salty, or sour flavors in favor of seeds, roots, and well-aged, mellow wines helps maintain the body's vital "dewy" moisture. He emphasizes that the quality of one's drink is the most important factor in transporting nourishment and hydrating the internal organs.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, diet, longevity, dewy juice, Manichaeans, nutrition, wine, Plautus, seeds and roots, hydration</keywords>

298 | The History of Life and Death.

<margin>Mild brews, and beer of the same kind.</margin>
**22** | **Mead** <note>original: *Hydromel*; a fermented drink made of honey and water</note> (as we judge) would not be bad for the health, provided it were strong and well-aged. However, since all honey contains something sharp <note>original: *Acutum*</note>—as is evident from that extremely biting **Spirit** <note>original: *Aqua*; literally "water," but here referring to a distilled acid or "spirit of honey"</note> which alchemists extract from it, which is powerful enough even to dissolve metals—it would be better if a similar drink were made from **Sugar**. In such a drink, the sugar should not be merely lightly infused, but thoroughly incorporated in the same way honey is in mead. This drink should be aged for a full year, or at least six months, so that the water may lose its "rawness" <note>the harsh, unrefined quality of untreated water</note> and the sugar may acquire a **refined quality** <term>subtlety: a state where the particles of a substance are finely divided, making it easier for the body to digest without causing inflammation</term>.

**23** | Furthermore, the **Aging** of wine or any drink has this effect: it produces a **refined quality** in the parts of the liquid, and [removes] the **Sharpness** <note>the text breaks off at *Acrimo-*, likely *Acrimoniam* or bitterness/harshness</note>...

<summary>Francis Bacon discusses the medicinal benefits of aged mead and suggests that a sugar-based fermented drink might be even healthier, provided it is properly aged to refine its qualities. He emphasizes that aging helps remove the "rawness" of water and the harshness of the ingredients.</summary>

<keywords>mead, sugar, fermentation, alchemy, distillation, aging of wine, digestion, subtlety, Francis Bacon, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "depurating" or refining beverages to make them less harmful to the body's internal "spirits," a central theme in Bacon's "History of Life and Death."</meta>

# *History of Life and Death.* [page] 299

...acrimony in the *Spirits*; <note>In Bacon’s theory, "spirits" are the volatile, active substances within liquids and living bodies that can cause either vitality or decay.</note> of which the first [kind of acrimony] is useful, but the second is harmful. Therefore, to unravel this complication, let there be put into the cask, before the wine has settled at all from the *Must* <note>original: "Musto"—the freshly pressed grape juice that still contains skins and seeds.</note>, *Pork or Venison*, well-cooked; this is so that the *Spirits of the wine* may have something to chew and gnaw upon, and thereby lay aside their biting nature.

Similarly, if *Beer* <margin>24</margin> receives not only grains of *Wheat, Barley*, *Oats, Peas*, etc., but also a portion (let us say, a third) of fatty *Roots or Pulps* (such as *Potato roots*, the *Pith of Orach* <note>original: "Medullæ Artiplicis"—Orach is a leafy vegetable also known as mountain spinach.</note>, *Burdock roots*, or other sweet and edible roots), the *Drink* will be more useful <note>Bacon suggests these additives make the alcohol less "sharp" and more nourishing, which he believed helped prolong life.</note>

[footer] V ad

<summary>This page describes methods for mellowing the "sharpness" of wine and beer by adding meats or fatty roots during fermentation. Bacon argues that giving the active "spirits" of the drink something to act upon—like pork or burdock—prevents them from being too harsh on the human body.</summary>

<keywords>fermentation, wine, beer, longevity, spirits, dietary health, potatoes, burdock, alchemy of food</keywords>

...as much as possible; especially if they are well incorporated through **Age**; Such things should be made from **Fruits** and sweet **Roots**; Namely, **Wines** and **Drinks** made from fresh **raisins**, **jujubes** <note>A date-like fruit used in early modern medicine for its soothing, nutritive properties.</note>, **dried figs**, **dates**, **parsnips**, **bulbs** or **potatoes** <note>original: "Potadis" — an early Latinization of the potato, which was still a relatively new import from the Americas at the time of writing.</note>, and the like, sometimes with an addition of **Liquorice**; Also, a **Drink** made from **Indian grains** (which they call **Maize**) with a **Mixture of Sweets**, is very beneficial. It must be noted, however, that the intention of **Preserving the Liver** in a certain Softness and Fatness is far more powerful than the other intention, which pertains to the **Opening of the Liver** <note>In 17th-century medicine, "opening" the liver meant clearing blockages to allow the healthy flow of bodily fluids, known as "humors."</note>; which points rather toward **Health** than toward the **Longevity of life**; except that those **Obstructions** which lead to **Parching** <note>original: "Torrefactionem" — a medical state where the organs become dried out or "scorched" by internal heat, a process Bacon associated with the decline of old age.</note> are equally...

<summary>Bacon describes the preparation of sweet medicinal drinks from fruits and roots—including then-exotic maize and potatoes—to maintain the liver's suppleness. ...nourishment <note>original: "mentationem," likely the end of "alimentationem" (nourishment)</note> is maintained; nevertheless, the Actions of the ***External Parts*** must also concur; so that just as the ***Internal Faculty*** sends out and thrusts forth ***Nourishment***; so the ***Faculty of the External Parts*** should seize and attract the same; and the weaker the ***Faculty of Concoction*** <note>In early modern medicine, "concoction" refers to the body's internal process of refining food and fluids through heat to make them usable for the tissues.</note> may be, the more need there is for the concurrent assistance of the ***Attractive Faculty*** <note>The "Attractive Faculty" was believed to be the power of individual organs or tissues to pull nutrients toward themselves from the bloodstream.</note>.

# OPERATION
## upon the Final Act 
## of <term>Assimilation</term>.
<note>In early modern science, "assimilation" referred to the biological process where the body transforms nutrients and external matter into its own living substance.</note>
### VIII.
________________________________________

<margin>Connection</margin> Concerning the Final Act of Assimilation (which the three immediately preceding operations aim toward), the instruction shall be brief and simple; and the matter requires explanation more than any varied instruction.

Com- <note>This is a catchword at the bottom of the page, indicating the start of the next word on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the eighth operation of the treatise, focusing on the final stage of "assimilation"—the process by which the body turns nourishment into its own tissue.</summary>
<keywords>assimilation, biology, nutrition, bodily restoration, Francis Bacon</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
*History of Life and Death.* [Page number:] 343

Discussion.

<margin>1</margin>
It is certain that all bodies are endowed with a certain desire for **a**ssimilating <note>The process by which a substance converts surrounding matter into its own nature.</note> those things which are in their vicinity. Those substances which are thin and <term>pneumatic</term> <note>In 17th-century science, "pneumatic" referred to "spirits" or gas-like substances believed to be the active agents in all matter.</note>—such as flame, spirit, and air—do this generously and eagerly. But on the contrary, those things which have a thick and tangible mass do so very weakly; this is because that desire for assimilation is restrained by a stronger desire for rest <note>original: "quietis". In this context, it refers to the tendency of dense matter to remain inactive or unchanged, similar to the later concept of inertia.</note>,

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "assimilation," where substances attempt to convert nearby matter into their own likeness, noting that active "pneumatic" spirits do this more effectively than dense, inactive matter.</summary>
<keywords>assimilation, pneumatic spirits, physics of matter, Francis Bacon, tangible bodies, desire for rest</keywords>

344 | *History of Life and Death*

[of ...ness], and of not moving itself, is bound.

2 It is likewise certain that the **desire for assimilation**, which is bound within the physical mass (as we have said) and rendered useless, is somewhat liberated and stirred up by heat or by a nearby <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "Spiritu"; in 17th-century science, "spirits" were seen as very thin, active substances trapped within matter that drove all change and biological processes</note>, so that it may finally be put into action. This is the only reason why inanimate things do not <term>assimilate</term> <note>original: "assimilent"; the process by which a living thing converts nourishment into its own substance</note>, whereas animate things do.

3 This is also certain: that the harder the consistency of a body, the more it [lacks/is...] <note>The text cuts off with the fragment "indi-", likely leading to "indigeat" (needs) or "indifferens" (indifferent) on the following page.</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon explains that the power of "assimilation"—the ability to transform matter into one's own substance—is latent in all matter but only activated in living things by the presence of "spirits" or heat. This serves as his fundamental distinction between the living and the non-living.</summary>

4 The act of **Assimilation** <note>original: "Actus Assimilationis"; the biological process by which an organism converts nutrients into its own living substance.</note> (which is triggered, as we have said, by the surrounding Heat) is a Motion that is remarkably Precise, Subtle, and active in the smallest particles. However, all motions of this kind are only in their full Strength when all **Local Motion** <note>Physical movement from one place to another, such as the body moving or fluids being shaken.</note> ceases, which might otherwise disturb it. For indeed, the Motion of **Separation into Homogeneous parts** <note>The natural process where similar substances within a mixture gather together; Bacon uses the example of cream separating from the rest of the milk.</note>, which occurs in Milk, because...

it is so, that the cream <note>original: "Flos"; literally "the flower," referring to the richest part of the milk that rises to the top</note> floats on top and the whey sinks to the bottom; this will never happen if the milk is gently stirred. Nor will any <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of decay or rotting, which Bacon believed was caused by "spirits" escaping from a stagnant substance</note> occur in water or in mixtures if they are kept in continuous local motion. From these premises which we have established, we may now draw this conclusion for our current inquiry:

The actual act of <term>assimilation</term> <note>The biological process where the body transforms nutrients into its own living tissue</note> is completed chiefly during sleep and rest, especially toward dawn, once the distribution of nourishment has already been performed. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the "final act of assimilation," which is the physiological process of the body turning nourishment into its own substance.</meta>

# 348 | History of Life and Death

<meta>This text provides an overview of text summarization in natural language processing (NLP), discussing its main approaches, techniques, and applications.</meta>

Text summarization is a natural language processing task that involves condensing long documents into shorter versions while retaining the core information. It is useful for summarizing articles, documents, or video transcripts. There are two primary approaches:

*   **Extractive Summarization:** This method identifies and extracts the most important sentences or phrases from the original text based on specific criteria like topic relevance. While effective and straightforward, the resulting summaries may lack cohesion.
*   **Abstractive Summarization:** This approach generates a new, concise version of the text by understanding its meaning rather than just extracting existing sentences. It is more complex and requires deeper language processing but typically produces more natural and informative summaries.

Additionally, **hybrid approaches** combine both methods, often by extracting key sentences and then using abstractive techniques to rewrite them more concisely.

Research in this field is ongoing, utilizing various algorithms such as:
*   **Frequency-based methods:** Ranking sentences based on word frequency.
*   **Latent semantic analysis:** Using statistical techniques to find relationships between words and phrases.
*   **Machine learning:** Training algorithms on large datasets to identify essential information.

Text summarization is a valuable tool for quickly grasping key information in fields like journalism, research, and business.

### *History.*

**1** The **living spirit** <term>living spirit: In 17th-century natural philosophy, this was a subtle, air-like substance believed to inhabit the bodies of animals and plants, driving their growth and movement.</term> seems to require three things in order to subsist: suitable **motion**; tempered **cooling**; and proper **nourishment**. **Flame**, however, seems to require only two of these: namely, **motion** and **nourishment**. This is because flame is a simple substance, while spirit is a composite one; so much so that if the spirit approaches too closely to the nature of flame, it destroys itself.

**2** Even a **flame** is dissolved and killed by a greater and more powerful flame,

<summary>This page compares the requirements of the "living spirit" in organisms to the requirements of a flame, noting that the spirit is a complex substance requiring cooling to survive.</summary>
<keywords>living spirit, vital spirit, flame, nourishment, respiration, Francis Bacon, physiology, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the extinction of life, specifically how the "spirit" (the vital force) is affected by compression, comparing it to the behavior of a flame.</meta>

# History of Life and Death | 373

...is killed, as <note>original: "vt benè notauit Aristoteles"</note> Aristotle well noted; much more so the <term>Spirit<note>In 17th-century science, the "Spirit" was considered a physical, vaporous substance—a mix of air and fire—that circulated through the body to provide life and movement.</note></term>.

3. A flame, if it is compressed too much, is extinguished; as can be seen in a candle when a glass is placed over it: For the air, expanded by the heat, squeezes the flame, diminishes it, and puts it out. Nor is a flame ignited in hearths if the fuel is packed together without some space left between.

4. Even glowing objects are extinguished by compression; just as if you were to forcefully press a glowing coal with a piece of iron or with your foot, the fire is immediately extinguished.

5. But to come to the Spirit; if blood or phlegm should rush into the <term>ventricles of the brain<note>The ventricles are the open chambers within the brain. Early scientists believed these were the command centers where the "animal spirits" resided.</note></term>, death occurs suddenly;

<summary>Bacon explains that the vital spirit, like a flame, requires space to exist and is extinguished if it is physically compressed or smothered by bodily fluids.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirits, compression, flame, Aristotle, brain ventricles, physiology, Francis Bacon</keywords>

In Francis Bacon’s natural philosophy, the 'spirit' was not a mystical concept but a thin, vaporous, and material substance that resided in the body and powered its functions.</note>
25 | An excessively heavy and continuous loss of blood—as sometimes occurs from hemorrhoids, or occasionally through the vomiting of blood when internal veins are opened or ruptured, or from wounds—brings about a swift death. This is because the blood in the veins supplies the blood in the arteries, and the blood in the arteries, in turn, serves the **spirit**. <note>Bacon is describing a hierarchical system of fuel: food becomes blood, blood becomes arterial blood, and arterial blood sustains the vital spirit.</note>
26 | The total amount of food and drink that a person consumes in a day (fed twice) is by no means small. It is far more than what is discharged through the stool, the urine, or through sweat. You might say, "This is no surprise," since the remainder is transformed into the **juices** <note>The 'juices' or *succos* refer to the bodily humors and nutritive fluids used for growth and tissue repair.</note> and the very substance of the body.

<summary>Bacon explains that death occurs when the "spirit" is no longer nourished by the blood, and he observes that the body retains the majority of consumed food to build its own physical substance.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, blood loss, physiology, digestion, nutrition, Francis Bacon, juices</keywords>

->*History of Life and Death.* | 385<-

...is seen. <note>original: "tur." This is the suffix of a word continued from the previous page, likely "videtur," meaning "it is seen" or "it seems."</note> Rightly so: But consider for a little <margin>8</margin> while that this addition occurs twice a day, and yet the body does not overflow; similarly, although the *Spirit* <note>Bacon uses "Spirit" to refer to the "vital spirits"—a subtle, active substance thought to animate the body and regulate its functions.</note> is repaired, it does not grow excessively in its *Quantity*.

It is of no use for *Nourishment* to be present in a <margin>27</margin> remote degree; rather, it must be of that kind, and so prepared and administered, that the *Spirit* can act upon it. For the wick of a candle will not suffice to maintain the flame unless wax is also present; nor can humans be fed on herbs alone. And from this arises *Senile Atrophy* <note>The physical wasting away of the body associated with old age.</note>, because even though flesh and blood are present, the *Spirit* has become so sparse and thin, and the juices and blood so exhausted and stubborn, that it [the spirit] does not hold...

Bb 4 | Pro- <note>The footer "Bb 4" is a signature mark for the printer; "Pro-" is a catchword for the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that the body's "vital spirits" require specific, high-quality nourishment to function, and that old age (senile atrophy) occurs when these spirits become too weak to process even available nutrients.</summary>

continually generated and extinguished, nor does it last for any significant length of time. For Flame does not behave this way by its own nature, but because it exists among hostile forces; for a flame within a flame <note>Bacon suggests that fire is only "momentary" because the surrounding air consumes it; if fire were surrounded by more fire, it would persist.</note> endures. But the **Living Spirit** <term>Spiritus Vivus: In Bacon's natural philosophy, this refers to a thin, vaporous substance—distinct from the soul—that animates the body's organs.</term> dwells among friendly surroundings and receives much support. Therefore, since Flame is a fleeting substance, while Air is a stable substance, the nature of the **Living Spirit** holds a middle position between the two.

<margin>2</margin> Concerning the death of the spirit through the destruction of the organs (such as occurs through disease or violence), that is not our present inquiry (as we stated at the beginning), even though that process also ends in the same three "little chambers" <note>original: *atriola*; Bacon refers to the heart, lungs, and brain as the three vital halls or courtyards where life is finally lost.</note>. Let these then be our findings concerning the **Form of Death** itself.

32 | Eels, snakes, and insects continue to move for a long time in their individual parts after being cut up; so much so that country folk believe the severed pieces are attempting to join together again. Even birds with their heads torn off will hop about for a while; indeed, the hearts of animals continue to beat for a long time after being removed. I personally remember seeing the heart of a man who had been eviscerated (a form of punishment used among us for traitors <note>original: "Supplicij Genere apud nos versus Proditores recepto" — This refers to the English legal sentence for high treason: being hanged, drawn, and quartered.</note>), which, when thrown into the fire according to custom, leaped upward—initially to a height of a foot and a half, and then gradually less and less. This lasted for a duration of (as I recall) seven or

# Front Cover: The Royal Binding

->**[Heraldic Emblem]**<-

This volume is bound in aged parchment <term>vellum: a high-quality writing surface made from calfskin, often used for luxury bindings</term> which shows the natural patina of several centuries. In the center of the front cover is a magnificent <term>supralibros: a coat of arms or heraldic device stamped in gold on the outside of a book's cover to indicate the owner's identity</term> stamped in gold.

The emblem features two shields placed side-by-side beneath a single, ornate royal crown, indicating a union of territories. 

*   **The Left Shield:** Represents the **Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth**, featuring the heraldic White Eagle and the "Pursuer" <note>original: "Pahonia", the traditional coat of arms of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania depicting a knight on horseback</note>.
*   **The Right Shield:** Represents the **Electorate of Saxony**, identifiable by its horizontal bars and a diagonal "garland of rue" <term>crancelin: a stylized, crown-like floral wreath used in German heraldry</term>.

A smaller shield placed at the very center likely contains a royal monogram, identifying the specific monarch—likely Augustus II the Strong or Augustus III, who reigned over both Poland and Saxony during the 18th century.

<margin>Faint, illegible handwritten marks appear near the top edge</margin>

The binding shows signs of heavy use, with staining and wear consistent with age. Along the left edge, one can see the remnants of leather or fabric ties. These were essential for vellum-bound books, as the material tends to warp or "cockle" with changes in humidity; the ties kept the book pressed flat and protected the pages within.

<unclear>Faint dark mark at bottom right</unclear>

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, featuring a gold-stamped heraldic emblem belonging to a King of Poland and Elector of Saxony, bound in traditional vellum.</summary>

-># History of Life and Death.<-

->## The Entrance.<-

<insert>O</insert>f life being short and **Art** being long, there is an old song and a familiar complaint. <note>original: "De Vita breui, & Arte longâ, vetus eſt Cantilena, & querela." This refers to the famous first aphorism of the Greek physician Hippocrates: "Life is short, and art is long; the occasion fleeting; experience fallacious, and judgment difficult."</note>
It seems, therefore, to be fitting that we, who apply ourselves with all our strength to the perfecting of the **Arts**, should also undertake a study on the **prolonging of Human Life**, with the favor of the Author of both **Truth** and **Life**. <note>Bacon refers to God as the divine source of both scientific truth and biological life.</note>
For although the life of mortals,

...to inquire into what can be done both outwardly and inwardly (which nevertheless share a sort of common vestibule <note>original: *atriolum commune*; a metaphor for the transitional stage or "waiting room" before the end of life</note> before the very moment of death <note>original: *articulum mortis*</note> is reached); we consider this to be closely related to our present inquiry. with difficulty, and for the worse: so that from that time, human bodies begin to undergo that torment of <term>Mezentius</term> <note>original: "Mezentij". Mezentius was a mythical Etruscan king described by Virgil as a tyrant who tied living men to rotting corpses as a form of execution. Bacon uses this as a metaphor for the way aging tissues "kill" the healthy ones they are attached to.</note>, so that the living die in the embrace of the dead; and those parts that are easily repaired, because they are coupled with things difficult to repair, begin to fail. <summary>This page uses the gruesome metaphor of the tyrant Mezentius to explain how aging, rigid tissues (the "dead") gradually overwhelm and stifle the body's softer, self-repairing tissues (the "living"). It distinguishes between "easy to repair" substances like blood and flesh and "difficult to repair" structures like bones and membranes.</summary>
<keywords>Mezentius metaphor, aging process, tissue repair, vital spirits, physiology, anatomy, Francis Bacon</keywords>

...however, as the organs of repair fail, they themselves are no longer conveniently repaired in the same way, but are diminished and finally fail. The cause of this life cycle is this: that the Spirit <term>Spirit: in early modern science, 'spirits' were thought to be invisible, vaporous substances that animated the body but also slowly "consumed" it from the within.</term>, like a gentle flame, is perpetually predatory; and the external air, conspiring with it, also sucks out and dries up bodies. Eventually, this destroys the body’s workshop <note>original: "officinam Corporis"</note>, its machines, and its organs, rendering them unfit for the task of repair. These are the true paths of natural death.

...must be well and carefully pondered <note>original: "benè & diligenter animo voluendæ"</note>. For truly, he who does not understand the paths of **Nature**, how can he encounter her or change her course?

   Therefore, there must be a two-fold **Inquiry**: the first concerning the **Consumption** or **Pillage** of the human body, and the second concerning its **Repair** or **Restoration**. The goal is that the former be hindered as much as possible, while the latter is strengthened.

   The first of these relates primarily to the **Spirits** <term>Vital spirits: In early modern science, these were thought to be thin, vaporous substances that lived within the organs and were responsible for both life and the gradual "drying out" or aging of the body</term> and the **External Air**,

16	*History of Life and Death.* <note>original: "Historia Vitæ & Mortis"</note>

*Inquire more diligently into the **Consumption of Inanimate Bodies and Vegetables**; and into the **Methods and Processes** by which they occur; and furthermore into the **Prevention and Delaying of Drying Out, <term>Arefaction</term>, and Consumption**; <note>Arefaction refers to the process of a substance becoming dry and brittle, essentially turning to dust.</note> and the **Preservation of Bodies** in their natural state; and again, into the **<term>Inteneration</term>, Suppling, and <term>Reviviscence</term>** of bodies after they have once begun to dry out.* <note>"Inteneration" is a favorite term of Bacon’s meaning the softening of hardened or dried-out tissues; "Reviviscence" refers to the restoration of life-like qualities or vitality to matter.</note>

*Yet, a perfect or thorough Inquiry into these very things should not be conducted here, since they ought to be drawn from their own specific Heading on **Durability**; <note>Bacon refers to his broader system of categorizing knowledge, where "Durability" is a separate category of physical science.</note> and since they are not the main subjects in the present Inquiry, but only provide light toward **Prolongation and Restoration**—*

<summary>This page sets out a plan to study how plants and non-living objects decay and dry out, suggesting that learning to "soften" or "rejuvenate" dry matter is key to extending human life.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, aging, desiccation, inteneration, preservation, scientific inquiry, biology, prolongation of life</keywords>

Inquire into those signs that appear and are observed during a state of **health**, whether they relate to <term>Physiognomy</term> <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or health status from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note> or other aspects.

Thus far, the Inquiry concerning **Longevity** and the **Shortness of life** has been established in an unmethodical and disorganized way; to this, it has seemed right to add a Methodical Inquiry, one that points toward **Practice** through specific <term>Intentions</term> <note>In this context, an 'intention' refers to a targeted medical or scientific objective—a specific path the physician intends to follow to achieve a result.</note>. These are of three general kinds. We shall propose more particular distributions of these Intentions when we reach the Inquiry itself. Those three general Intentions are:

1. The **Prevention of Consumption** <note>original: "Prohibitio Consumptionis"; referring to the wasting away of the body's vital moisture and heat.</note>; 
2. The **Perfection of Repair** <note>original: "Perfectio Reparationis"; the process by which the body restores its parts through nourishment.</note>; 
3. The **Renovation of Old Age** <note>original: "Renouatio Veterationis"; the attempt to actually renew or "make new" tissues that have already become hard or decayed.</note>.

Concerning those things which [affect] the Body in Man, <margin>12.</margin>
ab <note>This is a catchword, indicating the start of the first word on the following page.</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon transitions from general observations on aging to a rigorous, three-part scientific "Intention" for extending life: preventing decay, perfecting nourishment, and renewing old tissues.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Longevity, Physiognomy, Medical Intentions, Consumption, Bodily Repair, Renovation of Old Age</keywords>

4.      It has been observed that stones on the *Northern* side are consumed more quickly by the "eating away" of time than those exposed to the *South*. This is manifest in pyramids, temples, and other buildings. *Iron*, on the other hand, when exposed to the *South*, contracts rust more quickly, and more slowly toward the *North*; this is evident in those iron rods or grates that are fixed to windows. This is not surprising, since in all *putrefaction* <note>original: *putrefactio*; here used in the broader sense of chemical or organic decay</note> (such as *rust*), *humidity* accelerates *dissolution*, whereas in simple *drying out* <term>Arefaction: the process of becoming dry or parched</term>, it is *dryness* [that accelerates the process].

5.      In *vegetables* (we are speaking of those that have been plucked and are no longer growing), the *stalks*...

->History of Life and Death. | 31<-

The **spirit**, or **pneumatic body** <term>pneumatic body: from the Greek 'pneuma' (breath); a term used in early modern science to describe a subtle, invisible, and active substance that inhabits and animates physical matter</term>, is covered and enclosed by the tangible parts of the body. From that spirit, the beginning of all **dissolution** and **consumption** <note>original: *Dissolutionis, & Consumptionis*; these terms refer to the breaking down and wearing away of the body's physical substance</note> is taken. Therefore, the antidote to these processes is the **detention of the spirit**.

The spirit is detained in two ways: | 2. <margin>2.</margin>
either through tight compression, as if in a prison; or through a detention that is, as it were, spontaneous. And that **stay** <note>original: *Mansio*; refers to the spirit's act of remaining within the body instead of evaporating or escaping</note> is also invited for two reasons;

; namely, if the **Spirit** <term>Spirit: in 17th-century natural philosophy, 'spirits' were considered subtle, invisible, and highly active substances within all bodies that cause change, growth, or decay.</term> itself is not overly mobile or sharp; and if, moreover, it is less provoked to depart by the surrounding **Air**. Therefore, there are two types of **Durable** <note>original: "Durabilia"</note> things: the **Hard** and the **Oily**. The **Hard** constrains the Spirit; the **Oily** partly soothes the Spirit, and is also of such a nature that it is less provoked by the Air. This is because **Air** shares the same substance as **Water**, whereas **Flame** shares the same substance as **Oil**. ### _History._

<margin>13.</margin>
Herbs which are considered among the **colder** varieties are annuals, and die every year in both their root and their stalk: such as *Lettuce* <note>original: "Lactuca"</note>, *Purslane* <note>original: "Portulaca"</note>, as well as *Wheat* <note>original: "Triticum"</note>, and every kind of *Grain* <note>original: "Frumenti"</note>. Yet there are some among the cold plants which last for three or four years, such as the *Violet* <note>original: "Viola"</note>, *Strawberry* <note>original: "Fragaria"</note>, *Burnet* <note>original: "Pimpinella"; likely Salad Burnet</note>, *Primrose* <note>original: "Primula veris"</note>, and *Sorrel* <note>original: "Acetosa"</note>. But *Borage* <note>original: "Borago"</note> and *Bugloss* <note>original: "Buglossa"</note>, though they appear so similar while living, differ in their death; for *Borage* is an annual, while *Bugloss* survives beyond the year.

<margin>14.</margin>
However, most **hot herbs** endure age and many years; such as *Hyssop* <note>original: "Hyssopus"</note>, *Thyme* <note>original: "Thymus"</note>, *Savory* <note>original: "Satureia"</note>, *Winter Marjoram* <note>original: "Maiorana altera"; literally "the other marjoram"</note>, 
[space] C 4 <note>This "C 4" is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in order during binding.</note> [space] *Balm* <note>original: "Melissa"; Lemon Balm</note>.

<summary>Francis Bacon categorizes the lifespan of various herbs based on their humoral qualities, noting that "cold" plants like lettuce and wheat generally die within a year, while "hot" herbs like thyme and hyssop tend to live much longer.</summary>
<keywords>botany, longevity of plants, humoral theory, herbs, annuals, perennials, Francis Bacon, natural history</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 36, completing the word "corporis" (of the body).</meta>

->*History of Life and Death.* | 37<-

...of the body in ***Trees***, has something in common with longevity (all other things being equal <note>original: "cæteris paribus," a Latin phrase meaning all other factors remaining constant</note>); and similarly, the hardness of the material. Indeed, ***Trees*** that bear acorns <note>term: Glandiferæ; trees like the oak that produce mast or acorns</note> and those that bear nuts are generally longer-lived than those that bear fruit or berries. Additionally, late-maturing trees—whether in their fruit or their leaves, sprouting foliage later and shedding it later—are more durable in age than early-ripening ones. Furthermore, wild trees live longer than cultivated ones; and within the same species, those that bear sour fruit live longer than those that bear sweet.

***

### Observation

<summary>In this passage, Francis Bacon examines how the physical traits of trees correlate with their lifespan, noting that density, late-season cycles, and wild growth favor a longer life. He observes that nut-bearing and sour-fruited trees generally outlive softer, sweeter, or more "precocious" cultivated varieties.</summary>

<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, botany, tree lifespan, acorn-bearing trees, wild vs cultivated, late-blooming plants</keywords>

## Drying; the Prevention of Drying; and the Softening of what is Dried.

### The Account.

**F**ire and intense heat dry some things and melt others; <margin>Regarding Article 2, Point 1</margin>
    *As the mud hardens here, and this wax melts there,*
    *By one and the same fire.*
<note>original: "Limus ut hic durescit, & haec ut Cera liquescit Vno eodemque Igne." This is a famous line from the Roman poet Virgil’s Eclogues (8.80), used here to show how the same force—heat—can have opposite effects depending on the material it acts upon.</note>

Heat dries the earth, stones, wood, cloths, skins, and whatever does not flow. It melts <term>metals</term> <note>Bacon refers to "Colliquat," the process of turning a solid to liquid through heat.</note>, wax, gums, butter, tallow, and such things.

However
<note>The word "Attamen" (However) appears at the bottom of the page as a "catchword," a common printing practice of the time where the first word of the following page is previewed to ensure the binder puts the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the physical "history" of how heat affects different substances, distinguishing between materials that harden (dry) and those that liquefy (melt). This forms the basis for Bacon's theory on how the human body loses moisture over time.</summary>

<keywords>desiccation, heat, Virgil, melting, states of matter, inteneration, Francis Bacon, aging process</keywords>

2. | Nevertheless, in those very substances which **Fire** liquefies <note>original: *colliquat*</note>, if the fire is more intense, it eventually dries them out; for even **Metals**, under a stronger **Fire**, lose weight as their **Volatile** <term>Volatile: In 17th-century science, this refers to the lighter, "spirituous" components of a substance that are easily agitated and escape as vapor.</term> parts are released (excepting **Gold**), and they become more brittle. Likewise, those **Oily** and fatty substances, under a stronger **Fire**, become fried, toasted, more dry, and crusted.
__________________________________________________
3. | **Air**, especially open air, clearly dries things out and never liquefies them; this is seen when roads and the Surface of the Earth, soaked by Rains, are dried; when washed Linens exposed to the **Air** are dried; and when Herbs, Leaves, and Flowers are dried in the shade. Yet **Air** does this much more effectively if it is brightened by the Sun's rays (provided it does not bring in— <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains how intense heat and open air act as agents of drying, noting that even metals (except gold) lose weight and become brittle when their volatile parts are driven off by fire.</summary>
<keywords>desiccation, fire, air, metals, gold, volatile spirits, evaporation, heat, brittleness</keywords>

4. **Age** dries out <note>original: desiccat</note> most of all, yet most slowly; as occurs in all Bodies which become parched by Old Age (provided they are not first overtaken by Putrefaction). Age, however, is nothing in itself (since it is only a Measure of Time); rather, the Effect is produced by the **innate Spirit** <note>In Bacon’s natural philosophy, "spirits" are invisible, active substances within matter that drive change, movement, and eventually decay as they escape the body.</note> of the Bodies, which sucks out the Body's Moisture <note>original: Humorem</note> and flies away with it; and by the surrounding Air, which multiplies itself upon those innate Spirits and the Juices of the Body, and preys upon them.

And *Flowers*, such as the *Rose* and the *Lily*, are preserved for a longer time in earthenware <note>original: *Vasis Fictilibus*, meaning vessels made of baked clay or pottery</note> vessels that are well-sealed; yet the surrounding *Air* from the outside still has an effect, for it conveys and works its variations <note>Bacon refers here to the inconsistent changes in temperature or moisture in the environment</note> even through the vessel itself, as is clearly seen in the cases of *Heat* and *Cold*. Therefore, if the vessels are both carefully sealed and, once sealed, are furthermore buried under the *Earth*, that will be the best method. Nor is it less useful if they are buried not under the *Earth*, but under *Water*, provided that the waters are shaded, such as *Wells* and *Cisterns* inside *Houses*; however, those things placed under *Water* are better stored in glass vessels than in earthenware ones.
