*The softening of that which has been dried*, (which is the principal matter) provides few experiments; and therefore we shall join together some things which occur in Animals, and even in Man.

<margin>27</margin> Willow twigs, which are used for binding trees, become more flexible when soaked in water; similarly, the ends of fennel rods <note>original: "ferulæ"; giant fennel stalks were commonly used as canes or rods.</note> are placed in pitchers with water so that they do not dry out; indeed, even gaming balls <note>likely wooden balls used in lawn games or bowling.</note>, although they may have developed cracks through dryness, are filled up and made solid again when placed in water.

<margin>28</margin> Leather boots <note>original: "Ocreæ"</note> that have become hard and stubborn with age are softened by the rubbing in of tallow <term>tallow: rendered animal fat used as a lubricant or sealant</term> near a fire; even when brought near a simple fire, they soften somewhat: Bladders and membranes, after—

...to cure the <term>French Sickness <note>original: *Morbum Gallicum*, a historical term for syphilis</note></term>, and chronic **catarrhs** <note>original: *Catarrhos*, an excessive buildup of mucus in the nose or throat</note>, and **leucophlegmatia** <note>original: *Leuco-phlegmatiam*, a condition involving pale, flabby swelling of the body</note>, we use) leads men to extreme **emaciation**, having consumed the **juices** <note>Bacon refers here to the "vital juices" or humors that maintain the body’s moisture and health</note> of the body; which, after they have begun to be restored and repaired, are clearly seen to be more **youthful** and **fresh**; furthermore, we believe that **wasting diseases**, when afterwards well-cured, have prolonged life for many.

...see acutely, yet in the face of **Experience**—as if it were the bright light of day—they blink and go blind. <note>Bacon is arguing that while many scholars are intellectually sharp, they are overwhelmed and "blinded" by the complexity of actual physical evidence, preferring simpler abstract theories.</note> They speak of the **Elemental Quality of Dryness**; and of **Desiccants** <term>Desiccants: substances or processes that remove moisture</term>; and of the **natural Periods of Bodies** <note>The natural life cycles or timeframes in which objects and organisms exist</note>, through which they are corrupted and consumed; but meanwhile, they observe nothing of value regarding the **Beginnings**, the **Middle stages**, or the **Endings** of Drying and Consumption.

  2.    **Drying and Consumption,**

He argues for a more detailed study of the beginning, middle, and end stages of physical consumption.</summary>

Concerning **Thinning** <note>original: "Attenuatione"; the process by which a substance is made thinner, finer, or more fluid</note>, the matter is manifest; for the **Spirit** <note>In Francis Bacon’s natural philosophy, "spirits" were invisible, active substances contained within all matter that governed its growth and decay</note>, which is enclosed in every **Tangible Body**, does not forget itself; but whatever it finds in the body (in which it is besieged) that it can digest, work upon, and turn into itself; that it clearly alters and subdues, and from it multiplies itself and generates new **Spirit**. This is proven by that one piece of evidence, which stands for all: namely, that those things which are dried out the most decrease in **Weight**, and become **Hollow** and **Porous**.

7. | It must be noted that Fire and Heat dry things out only **incidentally** <note>original: *per Accidens*; in formal logic and early science, an "accident" is a secondary property or effect, rather than the primary essence of an action.</note>; for their essential work is to thin out and expand both the **Spirit** and the moist parts. **R**egarding the length and brevity of life in animals, the information that can be obtained is meager; observation of the subject has been negligent; and the traditions handed down are full of fables <note>original: "Traditio fabulosa"; Bacon frequently warns that historical accounts of animal lifespans are often based on myths rather than scientific evidence.</note>. In domestic <note>original: "Cicuribus"; referring to animals tamed or raised by humans.</note> animals, a degenerate life <term>corrupts</term> <note>Bacon suggests that living under human care, with unnatural diets and lack of exercise, alters and usually shortens an animal's natural lifespan.</note> the natural span; in wild animals...

14 | The **Cat's** lifespan is between six and ten years. It is an agile animal with a sharp spirit, whose seed (as <term>Aelian<note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman author whose works on natural history were widely cited during the Renaissance.</note></term> reports) burns the female; from which the opinion has grown that the cat conceives in pain and gives birth with ease. It is a voracious eater, which swallows its food rather than chewing it.

15 | **Hares** and **Rabbits** barely reach seven years; they are very prolific animals, even capable of <term>superfetation<note>original: "Superfœtantia" — the ability to conceive a second litter while already pregnant, a phenomenon observed in certain rodents and lagomorphs.</note></term>. They differ in this: the rabbit lives underground, while the hare lives in the open; also, the meat of the hare is darker.

*Birds*

It also highlights Bacon's reliance on classical authorities like Aelian for natural history observations.</summary>

Birds are much smaller in body size than four-legged animals <term>quadrupeds</term> | 16 : For an eagle or a swan is a tiny thing compared to an ox or a horse; likewise an ostrich compared to an elephant.

Birds are excellently covered | 17 : For feathers, by their warmth and their close fit to the body, surpass both wool and hair.

Birds, although they produce many offspring | 18, do not carry them all in the womb at the same time, but lay eggs in succession; thus, nourishment is more generously supplied to the fetus. <note>Bacon suggests that laying eggs one by one prevents the mother's resources from being spread too thin, unlike mammals carrying a full litter.</note>

Birds chew their food little, or not at all | 19, so that it is often found whole in their throats. Nevertheless, they crack the shells of fruits and extract the kernel. _History of Life and Death._ | 87

...be long-lived, the years are not counted. <note>original: "Anni non numerantur." This fragment concludes a thought from the previous page regarding the difficulty of determining the age of certain species.</note>

<margin>39</margin>
The life of fish is more uncertain than that of land animals, since, living under the water, they are less frequently observed. Most of them do not breathe <note>original: "Non respirant." Bacon is referring to the inhalation of air; he understood that gills served a different function than lungs.</note>; hence the *Vital Spirit* <term>Vital Spirit: in 17th-century science, this was an invisible, fluid-like substance thought to circulate through the body to sustain life and movement</term> is more enclosed. Therefore, although they receive cooling through their gills, that cooling is not as continuous as it is through the drawing of breath.

<margin>40</margin>
Since they live in the *waters*, they are immune to that *drying out* and *depletion* <note>original: "Desiccatione... & Deprædatione." Bacon believed the surrounding air "preyed" upon the body's moisture, leading to aging.</note> caused by the surrounding air. However, there is no doubt that the surrounding water, penetrating and absorbed into the pores of the body, does more harm...

<summary>Bacon discusses why the lifespans of fish are harder to document than land animals, focusing on how their "vital spirits" are cooled by gills rather than air. He notes that while water protects them from the drying effects of air, it may cause other types of bodily harm.</summary>
<keywords>fish, longevity, respiration, vital spirit, gills, desiccation, aquatic life, Francis Bacon</keywords>

There is also an ancient and trustworthy tradition concerning an ox lowing <note>the characteristic deep sound made by cattle</note> while being eviscerated <note>the removal of internal organs</note>. However, there is a more certain account regarding a man who was eviscerated by that kind of punishment (which we previously mentioned) <note>Bacon is likely referring to "hanging, drawing, and quartering," the contemporary punishment for high treason in England, where the heart was removed while the victim was still alive</note>. After his heart had been entirely torn out and was in the hand of the executioner, he was heard to utter three or four words of prayer. We have said this is more credible than the story regarding the *sacrifice* <note>Bacon is comparing judicial executions to ancient religious sacrifices of animals</note>; this is because the friends of such criminals are usually accustomed to pay the executioner a fee to carry out his duty with the utmost speed, so that the victim might be more quickly freed from their pain. In *sacrifices*, however, we see no reason why the *priest* would demonstrate such similar haste.

To resuscitate those who suffer from Fainting of the Mind <note>original: *Deliquia Animi*; referring to fainting or swooning fits</note>, or sudden Seizures <term>Catalepsy: a medical condition characterized by a trance-like state and rigid muscles, often appearing like sudden death</term> (of whom not a few, without help, would have even died), these are the methods in use:

<margin>34</margin> There have been many examples of men treated as if they were dead; *either laid out from their beds*, *or carried to their funeral*, or even *some buried in the earth*, who nevertheless returned to life. To grow to full size; then the state of peak strength in the limbs, and also of agility; to turn gray, and to go bald; the cessation of the monthly courses <note>Referring to menstruation</note>, and of the power of procreation; to decline toward decrepitude, and to become a **three-legged animal** <note>A classical allusion to the Riddle of the Sphinx; the "third leg" is a walking staff</note>; and finally, to die. <margin>2. These are the differences between Youth and Old Age.</margin> The differences between Youth and Old Age are these: The skin of the youth is smooth and spread even; in the elderly, it is dry and wrinkled, especially around the forehead and the eyes. The flesh of the youth is tender and soft; in the elderly, it is harder. Strength and agility belong to the youth; in the elderly, there is a lessening of forces and a slowness of movement. 398 | History of Life and Death.
***

**Weakness**: In the youth, the internal organs are soft and succulent; in the old man, they are salty and parched.

In the youth, the body is more erect; in the old man, there is a leaning toward a hunch.

In the youth, there is stability of the limbs; in the old man, weakness and trembling.

In the youth, the <term>humors|The four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament.</term> are bilious and the blood is hotter; in the old man, the humors are phlegmatic and melancholic, and the blood is colder.

In the youth, sexual desire <note>original: "Venus"</note> is ready at hand; in the old man, it is slower.

In the youth, the juices of the body are more dewy <note>original: "roscidi," referring to a moist, life-giving quality</note>; in the old man, they are more raw and watery.

In the youth, the <term>spirits|In early modern science, "vital spirits" were believed to be invisible, active substances that animated the body and transmitted sense and motion.</term> are abundant and swelling with vigor; in the old man, they are few and meager.

In the youth, the spirits are dense and fresh; in the old man, they are sharp and thin.

In the youth, the senses are lively and intact; in the old man, they are duller and failing.

In the youth, the teeth are robust and whole; in the old man, they are weak, worn down, and falling out.

In youth, the hair is naturally colored; in old age, regardless of its original hue, it turns gray.
In youth, there is a full head of hair; in old age, baldness.
In youth, the pulse is stronger and more rapid; in old age, it is fainter and slower.
In youth, illnesses are more <term>acute</term> <note>meaning sharp, intense, and short-lived</note> and curable; in old age, they are more chronic and difficult to treat.
In youth, wounds knit together more quickly; in old age, more slowly.
In youth, the cheeks bloom with color; in old age, they are either pale or deeply flushed, as the blood becomes thick.
In youth, there is less discomfort from <term>catarrhs</term> <note>original Latin: "catarrhis"; refers to the buildup of mucus or phlegm in the respiratory system</note>; in old age, the trouble is greater.

Nor do we know of any way in which the elderly improve (regarding the physical body), except perhaps in obesity. The cause for this is readily apparent: it is because the bodies of the elderly neither <term>perspire</term> <note>here meaning the release of metabolic "vapors" through the skin</note> well, nor do they <term>assimilate</term> <note>the biological process of converting food into living tissue</note> nutrients properly. For fatness is nothing other than an overabundance...

->## MOVABLE CANONS <term>Canons: In this context, these are fundamental rules or provisional laws of nature that Bacon established to guide his scientific inquiry.</term> on the Duration of Life, and the Form of Death.<-
____________________________________

### CANON I.

**Consumption <note>original: "Consumptio"; Bacon uses this term to describe the process by which a body’s substance is gradually worn away or depleted.</note> does not occur, unless that which is lost from a Body passes into Another Body.**

#### EXPLANATION.

There is no such thing as the total Destruction of Things: Therefore, that which is consumed either evaporates into the Air, or is received <note>The text ends here mid-sentence; the original Latin "recipitur" suggests the matter is taken up by an adjacent body or the surrounding environment.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the first of Bacon's "Movable Canons," establishing the physical principle that matter is never truly destroyed during the aging process, only transferred or transformed into other states like air.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, conservation of matter, consumption, natural laws, duration of life, physics of aging</keywords>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion from the previous page regarding the "escape" of spirits from matter and how to prevent decay by sealing a body from the air. The first line begins with the end of a word, likely "excipitur" (is received).</meta>

408 | *The History of Life and Death*
--- | ---
...is received into some adjacent body:
Wherefore we see a spider, or a fly, or an ant, buried and immortalized in **Amber**<note>original: "Electro"</note>, a monument more magnificent than any royal tomb; even though these are fragile and easily decayed things. This occurs because no Air is present into which any part of them might escape; and the substance of the Amber is so <term>heterogeneous</term><note>In this context, it means the amber is of a different nature than the insect, so it does not "leach" or absorb any of the insect's moisture.</note> that it absorbs nothing from them. We believe a similar effect would occur if wood, or a root, or something of that kind, were placed in **Quicksilver**<note>Liquid mercury</note>. Furthermore, Wax, Honey, and Gums have a similar effect, but only in part.

->## CANON II.<-

**I**n every tangible thing, there resides a **Spirit**, [enclosed] by the thicker **Body**.

<summary>This page explains how airtight substances like amber preserve fragile organisms by preventing their internal "spirits" from escaping. Bacon then introduces his second "Canon" or universal rule: that all physical matter contains a hidden, active spirit within its denser structure.</summary>
<keywords>preservation, amber, mercury, quicksilver, spirits of matter, Francis Bacon, decay, Canon II, entomology in amber</keywords>

No body known to us here on the upper part of the Earth is without a spirit <note>Bacon uses "spirit" here to describe a thin, active, gas-like substance trapped within all physical objects, rather than a soul in the religious sense.</note>; whether through the thinning <note>original: "Attenuationem"</note> and ripening <note>original: "Concoctionem" — a process of maturing or refining matter through heat, similar to digestion.</note> by the heat of the celestial bodies, or by other means. For the hollow spaces within tangible things do not admit a vacuum; instead, they contain either air or the spirit proper to the thing itself. 

Moreover, that spirit (of which we speak) is not some kind of abstract power, or energy, or <term>entelechy: A term from Aristotelian philosophy meaning the realized potential or "soul" of an object. Bacon is rejecting this metaphysical idea.</term>, or nonsense; but clearly a thin, invisible body;

## CANON III.

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's natural history of matter, specifically focusing on how "spirits" (invisible, active substances within matter) and "coarser parts" interact to cause changes like drying and melting. The Generation of Bodies. **Drying** <note>original: *Arefactio*</note> is not the proper work of the **Spirit** <term>Spirit: In 17th-century science, 'spirits' were thought to be invisible, highly active fluids trapped within all physical objects that governed their growth, decay, and movement</term>, but of the **Coarser Parts**, after the spirit has been emitted: for then those parts contract themselves, partly to **avoid a vacuum** <note>A reference to the Aristotelian concept of *fuga vacui*, the idea that nature acts to prevent empty space</note>, and partly through the union of **homogeneous parts** <note>original: *homogeneorum*; the tendency of similar substances to pull together</note>: As is clear in all things that dry out through **Age**; and in drier Bodies that are desiccated by **Fire**, such as **Bricks**, **Charcoal**, and **Loaves of Bread**. **Melting** <note>original: *Colliquatio*</note> is the pure work of the **Spirits**; nor does it happen unless they are excited by **Heat**; for then the Spirits, expanding themselves but not yet exiting, insinuate themselves and spread through the **coarser parts**; and they render those very parts **Soft** and **Meltable**; as in **Metals** and **Wax**: For indeed Metals and other tenacious things are suited for...

<summary>Bacon explains the mechanics of physical change, distinguishing between drying (the contraction of solid parts after spirits escape) and melting (the expansion of spirits within a solid).</summary>

# History of Life and Death. [Page] 409

...to restrain the Spirit <note>In Bacon's natural philosophy, "Spirit" refers to the active, invisible, and volatile substances trapped within all tangible matter that drive change and movement.</note>, lest it be excited and fly away. **Putrefaction** <note>original: "Putrefactio"; the biological and chemical process of decay.</note> is a **Mixed Work** of the Spirit and the Coarser Parts: For indeed, when the Spirit (which used to contain and restrain the parts of the thing) is partly released and partly weakened, all things are dissolved and return to their own **Heterogeneities** <term>Heterogeneities: The diverse, unlike substances that originally combined to form a complex body.</term>, or (if you prefer) their own Elements.

Whatever of the Spirit was within the thing gathers to itself (which is why decaying things begin to have a heavy Odor); the Oily parts gather to themselves (which is why decaying things have a certain Smoothness and Oiliness); the Watery parts likewise gather to themselves; and the Dregs <note>original: "Fæces"; the solid sediment or waste remains of a substance.</note> gather to themselves (from which arises that Confusion found in decaying matter). 

But **Generation**, or **Vivification** <note>original: "Viuificatio"; the process of coming to life or the formation of living organisms.</note>, is likewise a Mixed Work of the Spirit and the coarser parts; but in a far different way: For the spirit...

Dd 2 <margin>totally</margin>

### RULE 4.

IN all living beings there are two kinds of <term>spirits<note>original: "Spirituum." Moreover, it follows from this that whatever substances are fatty and oily remain for a long time in their original state. This is because the air does not readily "pluck" at them <note>The original Latin *vellicat* implies the air teasing or pulling apart the substance's structure</note>, nor do these substances themselves have much desire to combine with the air. Furthermore, the idea that flame is simply "ignited air" is completely false, as flame and air are no less different from one another <note>original: *heterogenea*</note> than oil and water. As for the statement in the Rule <term>Canon: a fundamental principle or law established by the author in this scientific treatise</term> that "Vital Spirits" <term>Vital Spirits: In Bacon's theory, these are the active, subtle substances that animate living things, as opposed to the "inanimate" spirits found in rocks or dead wood</term> more closely resemble the substance of flame, this should be understood to mean they do so more than "Lifeless Spirits" <note>original: *Spiritus Mortuales*; the innate spirits found in inanimate matter</note> do. It does not mean they are actually more like flame than they are like air.

->CANON VII.<-

The <term>spirit</term><note>In Francis Bacon’s natural philosophy, "spirits" are not ghosts, but extremely thin, invisible substances (like air or vapor) that reside within the pores of all solid objects and drive their physical changes.</note> has two desires: one of **Multiplying itself**; the other of **Departing**, and **Joining** with its kindred substances.

->EXPLANATION.<-

This Rule is understood to apply to **inanimate spirits**<note>original: "Spiritibus Mortualibus." Bacon distinguishes between the "lifeless" spirits found in all matter and the "vital" spirit found only in living things.</note>; for regarding the second desire, the **Vital Spirit** greatly dreads exiting from its own body. Indeed, it finds no kindred substances nearby in the immediate environment. It may perhaps rush to meet a desirable object at the outermost edges of its body; but—

<summary>Bacon explains that inanimate spirits seek to increase their volume and escape to join similar substances, while vital spirits in living things fear leaving the body.</summary>

CANON VIII.

**A** Spirit that is held back <note>original: "Spiritus Detentus"</note>, if it lacks the means to generate more spirit, will even soften the thicker, more solid parts of a body.

### EXPLANATION

The generation of a new **Spirit** <term>Spirit: In Bacon’s natural philosophy, 'spirits' were not ghosts, but extremely thin, invisible, and active fluids found inside all tangible bodies, responsible for both vitality and decay.</term> does not occur except upon those substances which are closer in nature to the spirit itself: such as moist things. Therefore, if the **coarser parts** (among which the spirit moves) are of a more distant nature, even if the spirit cannot fully transform <note>original: "conficere"</note> them, it nevertheless does what it can: it weakens, softens, and dissolves them. This is done so that even when the spirit cannot increase its own total quantity, it may nonetheless live more freely and dwell among materials that are more compatible with it.

This aphorism is extremely useful for our purpose, because it points toward the **softening** <note>original: "Intenerationem"</note> of stubborn <note>The Latin 'Obstinatarum' refers to hard or rigid tissues of the body.</note> parts by trapping the spirit within the body.

### CANON <term>Canon: A general rule or fundamental principle of science.</term>

<summary>Bacon explains how the 'spirits' within a body attempt to soften and dissolve hard tissues to create a more hospitable environment for themselves, a process key to his theory on preserving youth.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, biology, spirit, moisture, softening of tissues, vital spirits, 17th-century science</keywords>

->CANON IX.<-

**T**he softening <term>inteneration; the process of making hard or dry tissues soft and supple again</term> of the harder parts proceeds well when the spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; in 17th-century science, 'spirits' were considered invisible, active substances within matter that governed growth and decay.</note> neither escapes nor creates.

->EXPLANATION.<-

**T**his rule resolves the central problem and difficulty in the process of softening through the holding back of the spirit. For if the spirit is not allowed to escape, yet it preys upon the internal parts of the body, nothing is gained toward the softening of those parts in their actual essence; instead, they are dissolved and corrupted. Therefore, along with this holding back, the spirits must also be cooled and constricted <note>original: "astringi"; to tighten or compress the spirits to reduce their heat and movement</note> so that they do not become too active.

Ee CA- <note>This catchword indicates the next page begins with 'CANON X'.</note>

<summary>Bacon explains that preventing the body's 'vital spirits' from escaping is not enough to stop aging; they must also be cooled so they do not consume the body's tissues from within.</summary>
<keywords>softening, inteneration, vital spirits, preservation of the body, Francis Bacon, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

...into the Hard parts, rather than plundering the Soft; for the former it dries out, while the latter it softens. <note>Bacon is discussing the "internal heat" of the body and how it must be managed so it doesn't merely evaporate the body's fluids (the "Soft") but instead works to maintain the "Hard" structures like bones and membranes.</note> Furthermore, this same thing is effective for properly completing the process of nourishment; for such heat best awakens the **Power of Assimilation** <term>Assimilation|The process by which the body converts nutrients into its own living tissue</term>, and at the same time best prepares the matter to be assimilated.

The properties of this kind of heat ought to be as follows: 

First, that it be **Slow**, and not heat up suddenly. 

Second, that it be not very **Intense**, but moderate. 

Third, that it be **Uniform**, not irregular—that is, not constantly increasing and decreasing. 

Fourth, that if this heat finds something that resists it, it is not easily choked or weakened. 

This operation is quite subtle; but since it is among the most useful, it must not be abandoned. We indeed

## RULE XIV.

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's 'History of Life and Death,' specifically the 'Explanation' of a preceding Canon regarding the effects of uniformity versus variety on the preservation of bodies.</meta>

430          **History of Life and Death.**

EXPLANATION.

In **inanimate objects**, this rule holds true without fail; for **inequality** is the mother of **dissolution** <note>original: "Dissolutionis Mater"; Bacon suggests that when the parts of a material are uneven or inconsistent, they pull apart and decay more easily.</note>. In **living beings**, however, it does not apply so strictly. This is because we must consider not only the **consumption** <note>The gradual wearing away or "burning" of the body's resources.</note> of the body, but also its **repair**. Since repair is driven by the **appetites** <term>Appetites: In 17th-century science, this refers to the natural inclinations or "desires" of matter and organs to seek what they need to function.</term> of things, and these appetites are sharpened by **variety**, the rule is less rigid. Nevertheless, it should still be accepted to this extent: that this variety should be an **alternation** rather than a **confusion**—acting as a sort of **constancy within inconstancy**.

### The spirit is held within a body of solid structure, even if against its will.

->EXPLANATION.<-

All things dread a **dissolution of their continuity** <term>dissolution of continuity: a physical concept where a substance is broken or separated into smaller, non-connected parts; a "break" in the substance's unified form</term>; yet they do so according to the measure of their **density** or their **thinness** <note>original: *tenuitatis*; in 17th-century science, this refers to how "fine" or subtle a substance is, like the difference between steam and water</note>. For indeed, the more **thin** bodies are, the more they allow themselves to be forced into smaller and narrower **passages**; therefore, **water** will enter a passage that **dust** will not; **air** also, which **water** will not enter; and even **flame** and **spirit**, which **air** will not enter. Nevertheless, there is a certain **limit** to this; for even the **spirit** is not so overwhelmed by the **desire to escape** <note>Bacon often personifies natural forces, suggesting substances have "desires" or "appetites" to move or react</note> that it allows itself to be broken up too much, or driven into excessively narrow **pores** or passages. Therefore, if the **spirit** is enclosed by a **hard body**, or even one that is **oily** and **tenacious** <note>meaning sticky or highly cohesive</note>, (which...

<summary>Bacon explains that while subtle substances like air and "spirit" can pass through smaller openings than solids or liquids, there is a physical limit to how much they can be divided before they resist further escape.</summary>

(which is not easily divided) it is surrounded, clearly constrained, and as if imprisoned; it sets aside its appetite for exiting. Therefore, we see that metals and stones require a long age for the **Spirit** <term>Spirit: In Bacon’s natural philosophy, 'spirits' are thin, invisible, active substances found within all tangible bodies. The reasoning is similar for tenacious substances, such as gums, except that they are dissolved by a milder heat. Therefore, firm bodily juices, constricted skin, and the like (which are brought about by the dryness of food, by exercise, and by the coldness of the air) are useful for **Longevity**; because they surround the spirit with tight barriers, so that it may not escape.

## RULE XVI.

->In **oily** and **fatty** substances, the <term>spirit</term> <note>In Bacon’s natural philosophy, "spirits" were not ghosts, but a very thin, active, air-like substance trapped inside all matter; their escape or "evaporation" caused a body to dry out, age, and eventually die.</note> is willingly detained, even if they are not **tenacious**. <note>original: *tenacia*; here meaning sticky, gummy, or viscous materials that might physically trap a substance.</note><-

### EXPLANATION.

If the **spirit** is not irritated by the **antipathy** <note>A natural repulsion or conflict between different types of matter.</note> of the body surrounding it, nor "fed" by too much **similarity** to that body, nor stirred or provoked by an **external body**, it does not struggle much to escape. All these conditions are met by oily substances. For they are not as hostile to the spirit as **hard** things are; nor are they as closely related to the spirit as **watery** things are; and they do not harmonize well with the **surrounding air**. <note>Bacon suggests that because oil and air do not mix well, the air does not "pull" the spirit out of the oil as easily as it does from water.</note>

RULE
<note>Catchword for the following page.</note>

<summary>This page presents Bacon's sixteenth rule of natural philosophy, explaining that oily and fatty substances are excellent at preserving the "spirit" within matter because they do not provoke it to escape or evaporate.</summary>

History of Life and Death. 439

## EXPLANATION.

This **Rule** <term>Canon: a fundamental law or principle of science or nature</term> is more difficult in its practical application than in its understanding. For it is manifest that whatever penetrates effectively, yet does so with a "sting" or a "tooth" (such as all things that are **Sharp** and **Acidic**), leaves a certain trace of dryness and tearing apart wherever it passes; the result is that it hardens the juices and wrenches the parts of the body. On the contrary, those things which penetrate by mere **Subtlety** <note>original: *tenuitas*; referring here to the fineness or thinness of a substance’s particles</note>, as if stealthily and by insinuation, without violence, serve to bedew and water the body as they pass through. We have described quite a few details regarding these matters in the fourth and seventh **Operations** <note>Bacon refers here to specific "clinical" procedures or stages of treatment outlined earlier in his work</note>.

**Assimilation** <note>The biological process of a living organism converting nourishment into its own substance</note> is best achieved when all **local motion** <term>local motion: physical movement from one place to another, such as walking or limb movement</term> has ceased.

#### CANON XXVII.

**The frequent renewal of repairable parts also nourishes those that are less repairable.**

#### EXPLANATION.

We stated at the very opening of this History <note>Referring to the introduction of Francis Bacon’s "History of Life and Death," where he outlines his theories on aging.</note> that the Way of Death is this: that the more <term>repairable parts</term> <note>Bacon refers to "fluid" or "soft" parts of the body, such as blood and flesh, which are easily replaced by nutrition.</note> perish in the company of the <term>less repairable parts</term> <note>These are the "hard" or "dry" parts, like bones, membranes, and nerves, which Bacon believed became "toasted" or brittle over time and were difficult to restore.</note>; so that we must exert all our strength in the restoration of these less repairable parts. Therefore, guided by Aristotle’s observation concerning plants—namely, that the newness of the branches refreshes the trunk itself as the sap passes through—we have judged that a similar logic would apply if often...

<summary>This page introduces Canon XXVII of Bacon's rules for longevity, arguing that renewing the body's easily repaired parts can help sustain the harder, more permanent structures. ### CANON XXIX.

**The complication that both Consumption and Repair are the works of Heat is the greatest obstacle to Longevity.**

### CANON XXX.

**The cure of diseases requires temporary medicines; but the longevity of life is to be expected from systematic regimens.** <note>original: "Diætis". In this context, Bacon uses 'diet' to mean a 'regimen' or a total lifestyle program, rather than just what one eats.</note>

### EXPLANATION.

Those things that happen by accident <note>referring to sudden illnesses or external injuries</note> cease once their causes are removed. However, the continuous course of Nature is like a gliding river; it requires constant rowing or sailing against the current to make progress. Therefore, one must work regularly through **systematic regimens**.

Now, these regimens are of two kinds:
->Regimens<- <note>This word appears at the bottom of the page as a 'catchword,' a printing convention used to ensure the next page followed correctly.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Bacon's thirtieth canon, emphasizing that while medicine treats sudden illness, extending life requires a permanent, disciplined lifestyle or "regimen." He uses the metaphor of a river to show that nature naturally flows toward decay unless we actively "row" against it.</summary>

<keywords>Canon XXX, medicinal cures, longevity, systematic regimens, metaphor of the river, Francis Bacon, life extension</keywords>

RULE XXXI.

**T**he vital spirit <term>vital spirit: In the science of the 17th century, this was a subtle, air-like substance believed to flow through the body to provide life, heat, and movement</term> suffers immediate destruction when it is deprived of either motion, cooling, or nourishment.

EXPLANATION.

**T**hese are, of course, the three conditions that we previously called the <note>original: "Atriola Mortis"</note> Antechambers of Death; and they are the specific and immediate ways in which the spirit is affected. For all the organs of the primary parts of the body <note>referring to the heart, lungs, and brain</note> serve to ensure these three functions are performed. Furthermore, every destruction of the organs that is lethal <note>deadly</note> brings the matter to this point: that one or more of these three functions fail. Thus, all other causes of death are merely different ways...

<summary>Francis Bacon defines the three essential requirements for sustaining the "vital spirit"—motion, cooling, and nourishment—and explains that the failure of any of these leads directly to death.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, Antechambers of Death, physiology, causes of death, motion, cooling, nourishment, Francis Bacon, 17th-century science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s concluding reflections on his "Intentions"—the specific medical and philosophical strategies he has outlined for the prolongation of life. It transitions from the technical methodology to the ethical boundaries of his research.</meta>

->History of Life and Death. 199<-

...satisfy these intentions. We believe that while posterity may contribute a great many things in practice, not much can be added to the **intentions** <note>original: "Intentionibus." # Operation upon the Spirits, so that they may remain youthful and be renewed. I.

### *History.*

**T**he **S**pirits <term>Spirits: In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" were not ghosts, but extremely thin, vaporous substances—the "master-builders"—that flowed through the nerves and organs to animate the body and carry out its functions.</term> are the master-builders and workers of <margin>1</margin> everything that happens in the body. This is understood both by consensus... <note>original: "Spiritus omnium, quae in Corpore fiunt, Fabri sunt atque Opifices." <meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s inquiry into the "History of Life and Death," specifically focusing on how certain substances affect the "vital spirits" to prolong life or cause death. This section examines substances that cause the spirits to retreat or condense.</meta>

216 | History of Life and Death

Regarding <term>Flight</term><note>original: "Per Fugam." In Bacon’s theory, "Flight" refers to the process where the body’s vital spirits are forced to retreat or condense by certain substances.</note>, by far the most powerful and effective substance is Opium; and next, Opiates, and sleep-inducing medicines in general.

15 | The effectiveness of Opium in condensing the <term>Spirits</term><note>In 17th-century science, "spirits" were thought to be invisible, active vapors within the body that governed all movement and sensation.</note> is quite remarkable; since perhaps only three grains of it can, shortly thereafter, so coagulate the spirits that they do not return, but are extinguished and rendered immobile.

16 | Opium and similar substances do not put the spirits to flight because of their coldness (for they clearly have "hot" parts); rather, on the contrary, they cause a cooling effect because of the flight of the spirits. <note>Bacon is debating contemporary medical theory here, arguing that while opium feels "cold" to the body, it is actually chemically "hot," and the coldness is just a side effect of the body's internal spirits running away from it.</note>

17 | The flight of the spirits caused by Opium and opiates is best observed when they are applied externally; because the spirits immediately withdraw themselves and no longer wish to approach, but [mortify—]<note>The text ends on the catchword "morti-," indicating the next page likely begins with the word "mortificant," meaning to deaden or kill the sensation in a limb.</note>

<summary>This page explores the potent effects of opium on the body’s "vital spirits," explaining that it works by forcing these spirits to condense or retreat, which can lead to sedation or even death. Bacon argues that opium’s cooling effect is a biological reaction to the drug rather than an inherent quality of the substance itself.</summary>

**40** | Clear and pure air, which contains no soot <note>original: "Fuliginis" — referring to smoke, pollution, or burnt particles</note> before it is drawn into the lungs, and is less exposed to the sun's rays, densifies <note>In Bacon’s theory, "densifying" or thickening the vital spirits prevented them from escaping or consuming the body too quickly, thus lengthening life.</note> the spirits best. and it is the only thing that performs this.

58 | Indeed, we also knew of a **long-lived nobleman** who, immediately upon waking, daily ordered a **clod of fresh earth** to be placed under his nostrils, so that he might inhale its scent. <note>Bacon often cites the smell of fresh earth as a restorative; this reflects an old belief that the "breath" of the earth contained vitalizing properties.</note>

59 | There is no doubt that the **cooling** and **tempering of the blood**, through "cold" herbs—such as **endive**, **chicory**, **liverwort**, **purslane**, and the like—consequently cools the <term>spirits</term> <note>In the science of the 1600s, "spirits" were thin, vapor-like substances that flowed through the nerves and arteries, responsible for movement and thought.</note> as well. However, this occurs by a circuitous route <note>original: "per Circuitum"</note>; whereas **vapors** act immediately.

At developed length, the **condensation of the spirits** by means of cold has now been investigated. The **third way** of condensation, we stated, is through what we call **soothing the spirits**. The **fourth** is through the **calming of their eagerness** and excessive motion.

->Concerning<- <note>This is a "catchword," a fragment of the first word of the next page to help the printer.</note>

<summary>Bacon discusses how scents and cooling herbs affect the body's vital spirits, emphasizing that while diet affects them indirectly, inhaled vapors act instantly to "thicken" and preserve them. Whatever things are pleasing and friendly to the spirits <note>In 17th-century natural philosophy, "spirits" were viewed as thin, invisible fluids that circulated through the body to manage movement and sensation</note> have a soothing effect. One must also ensure that the body—which is being more fully nourished and is not being made thin by the types of diets we have described—does not neglect the *timely use of sexual activity* <note>original: "Veneris vsum tempestiuum"</note>; <margin>**72**</margin> this is to prevent the **spirits** <term>spirit: In 17th-century science, 'spirits' were thought to be an invisible, vaporous substance that animated the body and controlled its functions</term> from swelling too much, which would otherwise soften and destroy the body. Thus, the investigation into a moderate and, as it were, frugal abundance of the spirits is now concluded.

Next follows the investigation into the *restraint of the movement of the spirits*; <margin>**73**</margin> for movement clearly thins and inflames them. This *restraint* is achieved in three ways: through *sleep*; through the *avoidance of intense labor* or excessive *exercise*, and indeed all weariness; and finally, through the *restraint of the passions* <note>original: "Cohibitionem Affectuum"; this refers to managing strong emotions like anger, fear, or excessive joy</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the importance of managing the body's 'vital spirits' through moderate sexual activity and the 'bridling' of movement. <meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's "History of Life and Death," focusing on the psychological and intellectual habits—specifically "wonder" and "light contemplation"—that contribute to a long life.</meta>

<margin>252 91</margin>

# History of Life and Death.

Wonder and Light Contemplation contribute most significantly to the prolonging of life; for they hold the **Spirits** <note>Bacon refers to "vital spirits," which 17th-century science imagined as subtle, active vapors within the body that governed health and animation.</note> in things that please them, and do not allow them to be disturbed, or to act in a restless and peevish manner. Hence, all the observers of nature—who had so many and such great things to wonder at (such as Democritus, Plato, Parmenides, and Apollonius)—lived to a great age. Even the <term>Rhetoricians</term> <note>Professional orators and teachers of public speaking in the classical world.</note>, who only sampled matters and pursued the brilliance of speech rather than the deep obscurity of things, were likewise long-lived; such as Gorgias, Protagoras, Isocrates, and Seneca. And certainly, just as old men are for the most part talkative and wordy, so too do talkative people very often grow old: for it indicates a light form of contem— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "contemplation."</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that a sense of wonder and a light, pleasant engagement with intellectual topics—rather than stressful or deep study—preserves the body's vital spirits and extends life.</summary>
<keywords>longevity, wonder, vital spirits, contemplation, natural philosophy, rhetoric, Democritus, Plato, Seneca, health</keywords>

# Concerning the Operation upon the Spirits, <margin>99</margin>
## so that they may remain youthful and flourish anew, these things have been investigated:

We have performed this task all the more diligently because, regarding these operations, there is for the most part a great silence among Physicians and other Authors. Most especially, however, we focus here because the operation upon the <term>Spirits</term><note>In 17th-century science, "spirits" were considered subtle, invisible fluids that circulated through the body to carry out the functions of life, sensation, and movement.</note>, and their <term>renewal</term><note>original: "Recrudescentiam," meaning to make raw or fresh again, as in restoring a youthful state.</note>, is the most direct and efficient path toward the **Prolongation of Life**. This is due to a double shortcut: first, that the spirit works efficiently upon the Body; and second, that Vapors and <term>Passions</term><note>original: "Affectus," referring to emotions or mental states which were believed to physically alter the spirits.</note> work efficiently upon the spirits. *History of Life and Death.* <margin>263</margin>

[...the decay] of the body accelerates; therefore, the exclusion of *Air* contributes to the length of life.

The *second effect* <margin>2</margin> that follows the exclusion of *Air* is much more subtle and profound: namely, that an *enclosed body*, being prevented from *perspiring* <note>In this context, "perspiring" refers to the subtle escape of internal vapors or "spirits" through the pores of a substance, not merely sweat.</note>, retains its *shut-in spirit* and turns it back toward the *harder parts of the body*; whereby the *spirit* softens and tenderizes them.

The reasoning for this <margin>3</margin> was explained in the section on the *Drying Out of Inanimate Objects*; and it is a nearly infallible *axiom* <term>axiom: a fundamental principle or self-evident truth</term> <note>original: "Axiôma"</note> that *released spirit* dries out bodies, whereas *retained spirit* melts and softens them. Furthermore, it must be simultaneously *assumed* that *all heat properly thins* and *moistens*, and only by *accident* <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "by accident" means a secondary or indirect effect rather than the primary nature of the action.</note> [does it dry things out]...

<summary>Francis Bacon discusses how preventing the escape of internal 'spirits' by excluding air can soften the body's tissues and potentially extend life. He distinguishes between the drying effect of escaping vapors and the softening effect of retained ones.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, spirits, perspiration, desiccation, moisture, heat, air exclusion</keywords>

### History of Life and Death | 267

Contributing to the **Closing of the Passages** <note>original: *Meatuum*, referring to the pores of the skin</note> are: the coldness of the air itself; the exposure of the skin, which causes it to become hardened; washing in cold water; and **astringents** <term>astringents: substances that cause the contraction of body tissues, effectively "shrinking" the skin</term> applied to the skin, such as mastic, myrrh, and myrtle. | 8

This operation will be much more effectively achieved through **Baths** | 9—though used only occasionally (especially in the summer)—consisting of astringent mineral waters that can be safely applied. Examples include **chalybeate** <note>waters containing iron, thought to strengthen the body's exterior</note> and **vitriolic** <note>waters containing sulfates or metallic salts</note> waters; for these powerfully contract the skin.

As for the **Clogging of the Pores**; | 10 pigments and similar thick, greasy substances, and (which is the most convenient for use) **Oil** and **Fats**, are no less...

[Footer]

when asked how he had preserved himself, he is said to have replied: "On the outside with oil; on the inside with honey." <note>original: "Extrà, Oleo; intùs, Melle." This famous anecdote is often attributed to the philosopher Democritus or the centenarian Pollio Romulus, suggesting that moisturizing the skin and consuming honey are keys to a long life.</note>

The **Irish**, especially the <term>Wild Irish</term> <note>original: "Syluestres." In Bacon's time, this referred to the Gaelic Irish who lived in traditional ways in the forests and rural areas outside of English-controlled towns.</note>, <margin>14</margin> are even now very long-lived. Indeed, it is said that a few years ago the **Countess of Desmond** lived to her one hundred and fortieth year, and three times throughout her life grew a new set of teeth. <note>The "Old Countess" of Desmond (Katherine Fitzgerald) was a famous example of longevity in the 17th century; she was said to have died in 1604.</note> It is the custom of the **Irish** to rub themselves while naked before a fire with **salted and aged butter**, and as it were, to "pickle" or preserve themselves.

These same **Irish** were in the habit <margin>15</margin> of wearing **linens** and **shirts dyed with saffron**. Although this practice was introduced to ward off <term>putrefaction</term> <note>Bacon refers to the medical theory that the body’s tissues would "rot" or decay like organic matter if not treated with preservatives.</note>, nevertheless (at any rate) we consider it to have been useful for the **length of life**. For **Saffron**, out of all the things which

->History of Life and Death. 271<-

17 Above all, therefore, we judge that the use of **Oil**, whether of **Olives** or of **sweet Almonds**, for anointing the **Skin** from the outside, contributes to **Longevity**; and this anointing should be performed every **Morning**, when rising from **Bed**, with oil in which is mixed a little **Black Salt** <note>In 17th-century medicine, "black salt" usually referred to unrefined salt or salt mixed with specific minerals, believed to have more potent "heating" or "cleansing" properties than table salt.</note> and **Saffron**. However, the anointing should be light, applied with **Wool** or a softer **Sponge**, and not so much that it drips upon the **Body**, but only so that it moistens and coats the **Skin**.

18 It is certain that **Liquids** in a **Greater Quantity**, even **Oily** ones, draw something out from the **Body**; but on the contrary, in a **Small Quantity**, they are absorbed by the **Body**: Therefore, a light **Sprinkling** should be performed, as we have said; or the **Undergarment** <note>original: *Indusium* — a linen shirt or tunic worn closest to the skin.</note> itself should clearly be smeared with oil.

S 3 *To be objected* <note>original: *Objici* — this is a catchword, a common printing practice where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to help the bookbinder.</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that a light daily application of oil mixed with salt and saffron helps seal the skin, preventing the body's internal "spirits" from escaping and thus promoting long life. He specifies that small amounts of oil are absorbed by the body, whereas large amounts might counterproductively draw moisture out.</summary>
<keywords>longevity, skin care, olive oil, almond oil, saffron, Francis Bacon, vital spirits, 17th-century medicine, hygiene</keywords>

It might perhaps be objected that this **Anointing with Oil**, which we praise (though it has never been in use among us, and among the Italians it has fallen into disuse), was once common among the *Greeks* and *Romans* and part of their daily regimen; and yet, in those ages, men were no more long-lived. But it is answered most correctly: **Oil** was used only after **Baths**, except perhaps among **Athletes**; however, **Hot Baths** are as contrary to our <term>Operation</term> <note>Bacon uses "Operation" to refer to his specific scientific method or therapeutic process for extending life.</note> as **Anointings** are beneficial, since the one opens the <term>Passages</term> <note>original: "Meatus"; in early modern medicine, these are the pores or channels of the skin through which vapors escape.</note> and the other closes them. Therefore, a **Bath** without a subsequent **Anointing** is the worst thing; **Anointing** without a **Bath** is the best. Furthermore, it was applied more for **Luxuries**

<margin>20</margin> *Anointing with oil* contributes to *health* in the *winter* by *excluding the cold*; and in the *summer* it serves to hold in the *spirits* <term>vital spirits: the subtle, vaporous fluids then believed to animate the body and maintain its heat</term> and prevent them from *dissolving*, as well as to ward off the force of the *air*, which is most *predatory* <note>Bacon believed the surrounding air naturally "preyed" upon the body's moisture, leading to aging</note> at that time.

**22** | The first **Inconvenience** is that by suppressing Sweats, it may induce Diseases from those waste-related Humors <note>original: "Humoribus illis Excrementitijs" — the 17th-century medical theory that "humors" or bodily fluids carried waste that needed to be expelled</note>: For this, a Remedy must be applied through Purgatives and Enemas <term>Clysters: an early medical term for an enema used to clear the bowels</term>, so that Evacuation is properly managed; For it is certain that Evacuation through Sweating generally benefits immediate Health, but works against Longevity. Moderate Purgatives, however, act upon the Humors, not upon the Spirits <note>In Bacon's philosophy, "spirits" are the vital, airy substances that inhabit the body and cause aging as they escape; he believed sweating depleted these essential forces more than other forms of waste removal</note>, which is what Sweating does.

not only <term>putrefaction</term><note>The process of rotting or decay.</note> but also <term>arefaction</term><note>original: "arefactioni." This refers to the process of the body drying out or withering, which Bacon identified as a primary cause of aging and death.</note>; and it would be most effective for prolonging life.
In this matter, however, several cautions must be
applied: First, that a very precise <term>comminution</term><note>The act of grinding or pulverizing a solid substance into extremely fine, microscopic particles.</note>
be performed; Second, that these types of hard and solid
substances be entirely free of any harmful quality,

->T 2<-

<summary>Bacon argues that if finely ground minerals could be integrated into the blood, they might prevent the body from drying out and decaying, thereby extending life. He emphasizes the need for these substances to be perfectly pulverized and non-toxic.</summary>
<keywords>longevity, blood, arefaction, vital spirits, pharmacology, mineral medicine, Francis Bacon</keywords>

286	*History of Life and Death.*

...skill <note>The text begins with "pertia," the suffix of a word like "imperitia" (unskillfulness) or "peritia" (skill) from the previous page.</note>, lest when they are dispersed and hidden in the veins, they cause some harm: Third, that they should never be taken with food, nor should they be ingested in such a way that they linger for a long time, lest they create dangerous obstructions around the **mesentery** <term>mesentery: the fold of membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall and holds them in place</term>. Fourth, that their use should be infrequent, so they do not gather and accumulate within the veins.

9	Therefore, the method of taking them should be on an empty stomach, in white wine mixed with a little almond oil, and physical exercise should be performed after drinking them.

10	As for the **simple substances** <term>simplicia (simples): medicinal ingredients, such as herbs or minerals, used in their natural state rather than in complex compounds</term> that fulfill this purpose, gold, pearls, and coral may stand in place of all others; for all metals, except for gold, are not without a malignant <note>The sentence ends abruptly, likely continuing with "quality" or "nature" on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes Francis Bacon's rules for safely ingesting medicinal substances like gold and pearls, emphasizing that they must be taken on an empty stomach with exercise to avoid clogging the veins or organs.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, medicinal gold, pearls, coral, mesentery, almond oil, exercise, pharmaceutical safety</keywords>

But in our judgment, the use of **woods**    11
in **infusions** and **decoctions** <term>decoction: a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling a substance, especially a medicinal plant</term>

since they can be taken in **food** and **drink**;
whereby they will more easily find **entry**
into the **veins**, and will not be deposited
in the **waste**.

...is suspected by us. And let these be the investigations concerning the Operation upon the Blood.

->**OPERATION**<-
->**upon the Juices of the Body**<-
->**IV.**<-

*History.*

1     There are two *Kinds of Bodies* (as has already been stated in the *Inquiry regarding Inanimate things* <note>original: "Inquisitione de Inanimatis"; this refers to Bacon's earlier scientific studies on non-living matter</note>) which are consumed <note>Bacon uses "consumed" to mean the process of being used up, dried out, or decayed by the body's internal "flame" or spirits</note> with greater difficulty: *Hard things and Fatty things*; as is observed in *Metals and Stones*, and also in *Oil and Wax*.

2     Work must therefore be done, so that the *Juice* <note>original: "Succus"; in 17th-century science, this referred to the vital fluids or "humors" that nourish the organs</note>
                    T 4 <margin>Signature mark for the printer</margin>

<summary>This page marks the transition in Bacon’s "History of Life and Death" from studying the blood to examining the "juices" or fluids of the body. He establishes a principle that hard and fatty substances resist decay better than others, suggesting this as a model for preserving human health.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, longevity, bodily juices, humors, anatomy, consumption, fats, metals, 17th-century science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the physical qualities of the body that contribute to longevity. It focuses specifically on the "hardness" of the body's substance and the types of food required to maintain it.</meta>

290 | *History of Life and Death*
---|---
 | ...that the substance of the Body should be **Somewhat Hard**; and also that it be **Somewhat Fatty**, or **Somewhat Dewy**. <note>original: "sub-roscidus"; Bacon uses "dewy" to describe a healthy, youthful moisture within the tissues that prevents them from becoming brittle or dry.</note>
**3** | As for **Hardness**, it is achieved in three Ways: by a *Firm Nature of Nourishment*; by *Cold condensing the Skin and Flesh*; and by *Exercise* fermenting and binding the juices together, so they are not soft and frothy.
**4** | Regarding the *Nature of Nourishment*, it ought to be such that it is less *dissipable* <term>Dissipable: prone to being easily scattered or evaporated. Bacon believed that "thin" juices were lost too quickly, leading to aging.</term>; such as are **Beef**, **Pork**, **Venison**, also the **Meat of Roe Deer**, **Kids**, **Swans**, and **Geese**, and **Wild Wood-pigeons** (especially if such Meats have been moderately Salted); likewise **Fish** that are Salted and dried; also aged **Cheese**, and the like.
 | [right-aligned] As for <note>This is a catchword, "Quoad" in the original, indicating the start of the next page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, Francis Bacon argues that a longer life is supported by a "hard" and "fatty" body consistency, which can be cultivated through cold temperatures, exercise, and a diet of heavy, salted meats and fish that are less likely to "evaporate" from the body.</summary>

<keywords>longevity, diet, physiology, beef, preservation of health, Francis Bacon, animal meats, body consistency</keywords>
